PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

SGBOOLMASTER : "What is the best conductor of iesand?" Small Boy: "Please, sir, a tom-cat, on the tiles." THE FORCE OF HABIT. —" Now, wifey, begin with your curtain lecture, else I shan't get to Sleep. EASIEST WAY TO MARK TABLE-LINEN. —Leave a baby and some black-current jam alone at the table for three minutes. THE OTHEL WAY. Chiribel "Do you think I would marry Smith if I wanted to spite Jones ?" Marie: "Certainly not. You'd marry Jones." MRS. YOIINGWED (anxiously) : "Do you think darling baby's lungs are sound, John?" Mr. Youngwed (sadly) : "Alas I yes; they're all sound!" . GREENLAND has no cats. How thankful the Greenlanders should be. Imagine cats in a country where the nights are six months long. AN AWKWARD MIST AKE. —Gent : "I must say, madam, that you carry your age remarkably well; you look almost as young as your daughter !" Lady : "Why, lam the daughter !" A CONTEMPORARY mentions a case beyond the ordinary oculist. It is that of a young lady who, instead of a pupil, has a college student in her ' eye. " MIGHT I ask who lives here ?" asked a polite ,gentleman of a stranger he met in front of a hand- .soo(o mansion. "Certainly, sir," as politely es- ponded the other. " Who is it, sir ? ' "I'm sure I. don't know," replied the stranger. PHILANTHROPIST : " Wha t's the matter, my man ? " Dejected Individual : "I am one of those whose employment has been taken from them by the fashion of woman's dress." " What is your business?" "Street-sweeper." JOHNSON: "When I do marry I intend to marry a sensible g•rl, if Pca.n find one. Tonison : "Now, there's Miss Sharpe ; she gave me rip.' Johnson : "Just the girl I want. Won't you introduce

me?" MAN is awfully clever in some things, but nobody has ever discovered one who could jam a hat-pin clean through his head and make it come out at the other side, as the women do. " Doc Ton, when do you think a man weighs most ? " asked a patient who was undergoing a course of dietary treatment. " When he steps on my corns," answered the doctor. CAPITAL" CHAT.

There can be no doubt that the Conservative leaders have made a serious blunder from their own point of view in connection with the •` Union- ist "squabble over the Leamington election. It is certain that when Mr. Balfour wrote his letter in favour of the Liberal Unionist claims he had no idea of the intensity of the Conservative feeling in the matter in the locality. Mr. Chamberlain had had an interview with him at which, no doubt, he , had placed the Unionist case in the most favourable light possible, and Mr. Balfour was misled. It is I too late to recall the letter, and the only way in which it now appears possible to patch up the I quarrel is to induce the Liberal Unionist candidate quietly to leave the field. Meanwhile, the whole businessAis causing very great bitterness at head- quarters, and the Liberal Unionists are openly com- plaining that they have beer made the victims of sharp practice.

In spite of reports which have been circulated to the contrary, it may, I understand, be regarded as practically certain that the Local Veto Bill will be brought in before the Easter holidays come round. Probably,indeed, it will be the last important legis- lative business before the adjournment. The Bill is ready for introduction, and the Government have nothing to fear on this stage. The Conserva- tives threaten to divide the House on the first reading, but they will get no support either on the Liberal or the Irish benches, while several Liberal Unionists will certainly vote with the Government if a division is taken. The Chancellor of the Ex- chequer is known personally to attach great im- portance to getting the Bill before the House of Commons as early as possible.

The total output of coal from the mines of the United Kingdom last year was 188,277,525 tons, or 2,798,399 tons more than the highest output hither- to recorded. There are no less than 705,240 per- sons employed in and about these mines, 80 per cent, of whom are employed below ground. The death-rate from accidents amongst those employed underground last year was 1182 per 1.000, as nom pared with 1 709 in .1893.

Those who remember the enthusiasm displayed some three years ago at the meeting of the West- minster Palace Hotel for the promotion of the Man- ning Memorial Fund cannot fail to be surprised at the comparatively small amount which has been realised and the consequent failure of the excellent plans then formulated, more especially as it was understood that the colonies and Continental countries had promised co-operation. The sum of .£6,457 can scarcely he considered an adequate re- cognition of the great services rendered by the late Cardinal to the cause of Roman Catholicism not only in England but all ever the world. The intention of the promoters to found a refuge in the East of London for outcast youths without distinc- tion of race or creed has to be shelved, and the name of Manning is to be perpetuated merely in a new ward of Nazareth House and a few beds in an old-established East End Refuge. It must,bowever, be remembered that the Cardinal's episcopal "silver jubilee," which occurred about eighteen months belore his death, was made the occasion of a very handsome presentation. This in great part he invested to provide anditional income for his successors. But even so, the aggregate of the two sums falls considerably short of the amount sub- scribed to the Wiseman Memorial Fund.

By the side of the great boot and shoe lockout: and strike, the lockout of the London mantle- makeis is a small matter, but it will add a good many more thousands to the ranks of the unem- ployed in the metropolis. One of its leading fea- tures is that it will chiefly affect the poor Jewish workers of the East-end. What is still more im- portant about it,however, is the principle involved, which is, whether the present sweating system by which these Jewish workers are ground to the dust is to continue unchecked or to be curbed and would be too much to say suppressed. The bulk of the sweating is, as usual, done by giv- ing out work to be executed at heme, and here is a case where the passing of Alr.Asquith's Bill is much wanted. If that measure is not choked off in the House of Commons it will have an easy passage in the House of Lords, and then the employers who are now ordering a lockout will have to bow their heads to the inevitable, and regulate their work by I the higher standard of the public conscience as embodied in the Horns Secretary's•Litl.

The Conservative party in the House of Commons did not spend a happy afternoon on Wednesday in the debate on Mr. Healy's Municipal Franchise ( Ireland) Bill. It has shown how shallow are its oft-repeated protestations of a desire to see the same laws and privileges accorded to Ireland as to England. and it has maladroitly made the pro- visions of Mr. Luttrell's Justices Bill the opportu- nity for showing distrust of self-government and heaping insult on the authorities charged wit n the ' administration of local affairs. The Irish Bill is, as Mr. John Morley showed, a modest measure, that has for one and twenty years been flouted by a Legislature always prone to turn a deaf ear to Ire- land's request for even small concessions to justice and equality. There was so little reality in the op- position to its second reading that it looked like sheer " cussedness " on the part of Ulster Tories, such as Mr. Dunbar Barton and Mr. Macartney to rail against conferring the municipal franchise on all ratepayers ; and as the Bill was read a second time and referred to the Standing Committee with- ' out a division, they incurred the reproach of block- ing their country's reasonable desires quite gratuitously.

"Poppyland," on the Norfolk coast, has been sug- gosied as a remedy for insomnia, and I believe the offer has been made to place at Lord Itosebery's disposal a mansion at Overstrand, near Cromer. There is ground, therefore, for believing that a pro- longed rest may be necessary for his lordships' complete restoration to health. So far as the Prime Minister is personally concerned, he is absolutely hopeful of recovery within a few weeks. A friend who spent the afternoon with him on Wednesday tells me that his spirits have greatly improved within the last. few days, and that his general con- dition is more hopeful.

The controversy over the Speakership continues with unabated vigour. The situation does not show much change. It is, however, now possible to state definitely that Sir Matthew White Ridley will not be nominated by the Government,and that a selection will be made from the ranks of the Minis_ terial supporters. On all sides gossip con miles to centre itself on the two names—Sir, Frank Lockwood and Mr. Gully. In Libera' circles it is felt that either would suit the position admirably, but the difficult doty of selec- tion may well be left to the Cabinet to discharge. sir Frank Lockwood has against him an indisposi- tion to resign the Solicitor Generalship.

The scare that was raised on Wednesaay 'about Lord Rosebery's .healtil has been overdone. It seems to have arisen from Mr. Murray,his lordship's secretary, having translated into non-professional terms a bulletin which should have been written in medical phrase. Nobody is more surprised at the exaggerations current than Sir W. Broadbent. His expressed opinion was that Lord Rosebery had improved, but not sufficiently to warrant his returning to any but urgent work. Sir William Broadbent went so far as to say that his patient is still weak, and that it will be better for him to remain at the Durdans some time longer to gain strength before being moved to the seaside, where the strong air might prove too sudden a change. If Mr. Murray bad seven or eight journalists clam- ouring at the doors at Downing-nreet on Wednes- day to learn bow Lord Rosebery was, his alinement the previous night is alone to blame. There is. according to Sir William Broadbent, no ground for alum, but much seed of care.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY. MARCH 23. 1895. LADIES' LETTER.

It is calculated that some ten million coloured photo- graphs of the Queen, the Prince and the Princess of Wales are produced annually, and find a ready sale all over the world, even in kinerica. There lives a man in London who makes his living by selling to those who are hard up, cr who want to float companies, the names and addresses of wealthy people. His charge is one pound a thousand. The Russian photographers have a strange way of punishing those who, having received their photos, do not pay their bills. They hang the pictures of the delinquents upside down at the entrance to their studios.

That Fatal Monotony in Homo Dishes ! "There are people who imagine that cookery is too practical and plebeian a pursuit to engage the attention of cultivated minds. It deals entirely with the body, they think, and not at all with the soul or spirit. When we are very young " (says Mrs. Flumphry in the March number of the "Windsor Magazine") "we think such thoughts as these. Experience teaches us the fallacy of such theories. Not even the most cultivated of women should despise the science of cookery, and I do not think it is putting it too strongly to say that, unless circu in- stances enable the house-mother to command the best skill in others, she cannot truly perform her duties to her children unless she herself knows intimately some- thing ot the practice of it. From infancy the little beings are helpless in her ht nds, and unless she under- stands the needs of the little hungry, healthy, growing bodies, she is liable to make mistakes which, later on, no one will more bitterly regret than she herself. Adults can usually take care of themselves, but even with them the house % ife should be on her guard against that fatal monotony of menu which robs food of much of its value. Many a man gets so heartily tired of baton for break- fast, for instance, that he eats it merely mechanically and without that pleasing of the palate which does so much to make it degestible. The world is full of flavours, but in most households but a very limited number are used. And when one thinks of the thousands of British homes where food is daily laid before the family in an ill-cooked, unpalatable form; one realises how the good gifts of a kindly Providence are mislaid or misused."

Ladies as Living Advertisements: Profits of " Dizin-

ter—ested " Recommendation.

"Look here! I know you are always on the look-out for particulars of strange and out-of-the-way callings and methods of making money. What do you think of this ? " It was in these words that the manager of an establishment devoted to the sale of fashionable dress fabrics recently called the writer's attention to an epistle which, written upon a little sheet of heliotrope- scented note-paper, ran as follows "DEAR SlR,—The writer, a lady of considerable business ability and moving in good society, would be glad to learn what commission your firm would allow on any accounts that might be opened upon her recommen- dation. She feels quite sure that on advantageous terms she could 'influence' a good many customers. In any event, she relies upon Messrs. So-and-so's good faith to keep this application .Secret." Some little while after the perusal of this interesting epistle, the writer of an article in "Cassell's Saturday Journal," whose curiosity had been greatly aroused by it, found an opportunity of personally interviewing the enterprising applicant, and gleaning some details as to her methods of doing business. "I have made quite a nice little income in this wa'y for some time past," she explained, quite candidly. 'My sister, you see, is in a large wholesale milliner's, where, however, when a subAantial retail customer is recommended to them, they do not disdain the opportunity for business; and she, having arranged a little commission for me on one or two sales made on my recommendation the idea occurred to me that the principle might be conveniently extended to other branches of trade. I soon had come to terms with several other people—a perfumer, bootmaker, jeweller, and corset-builder amongst the rest—until now there is hardly an article of personal adornment that can be referred to within my hearing of which lam nit able to cordially recom- mend a particular make, and, if my advice is taken, on which I do not reap a more or less valuable pecuniary reward. Of course, it wants to be very carefully done indeed. The slightest suspicion that my advice was ' interested,' and I should be boycotted—as the term is —at houses where at present I am a frequent and wel- come guest. One duty my profession—if so I may term it—eentails upon me, which, after all, is a very pleasant one, I must always be immaculately costumed,gloved, and shod ; and when, by some particularly ' takine, ' article of my attire I can attract the attention of some of my friends, half the battle is won. Only a week or so back, one Saturday afternoon up the river, a young lady whom I have known a short time exclaimed to me —• Oh ! what a darling little hat you have on again. Wherever do you get them ? They are always so exquisite !' Needless to say, as a great secret ' I whispered the name of the firm to her. • Thank you, dear, so much ! Should you mind if I had one like it ? No. I was sure you wouldn't. You are always so generous in saying where you get all your nice things.' When I got home that night I sent the firm a line that Miss B— would call upon them, and at the end of the month shall draw my little bit of commission on the substantial cheque they received from her for the goods they supplied.' .

MY DBAIIEST NELL'S,

We are all getting spring dresses and cloaks now, so of course you will want a few hints about what is likely to be worn. I must tell you, then, that I have come to the conclusion that capes are to be all the rage. Do you kr ow, I arn rather sorry for this, as I think from an artistic point of view nothing is equal to the jacket. Yes, Napes are to have things all their own way, and the appliqué upon cloth cut into holes so as to show a lining of a different colour, is seen a good deal, and combinations such as hlaok and white, blaok and heliotrope, and black and dark green look as well as any I have seen at present.

A very stylish coat may be made in satin faced arnaz)n cloth in the new R iyal blue tint, trimmed with black military braid. A very sinsru effect would be produced if the upper lapel or collar of the coat was of white cloth, finely braided with black. A blaok or dark rod satin blouse would be nice for wearing under the coat, or one of the new fancy silks in tiny shot checks of blue and red.

In bats there is still plenty of variety, and this is a fashionable one. It is of grey felt, matching the dress, and has two small clusters of black tips in front, with a spiky bow of rose-pink velvet, attached by a long narrow crystal slide. A knob of the velvet is placed at the back. To make the bow, out the velvet in the required shape for each spike, and a piece of fine muslin for lining; stitch these neatly up, turn the spike very carefully to the right side, and straighten lb out by thrusting a pointed penholder up it to the very top. If properly made, it will then stand upright without further trouble. Some very pretty hats are to be seen trimmed with ostrich feathers.

One of the prettiest bonnets one has seen is in sequin jet embroidery over gold tinsel, trimmed with fine white French lace, vim= rose-coloured roses, diamond oaboohons, and iridescent green wings. It may be worn with or without strings, but strings to this style of bonnet is more becoming.

Evening dresses remarkable just now for the sleeves being of a different colour to the bodice. This make some very nice effects. For instance, in the one I am going to describe to you, the skirt and sleeves of amethyst velvet are contrasted with a bebe bodice of primrose chiffon, this trimmed with a passementerie of shaded mauve and violet beads, and with braces of mink fur. The full, plain skirt consists of velveteen, or silk velvet may be employed. There is a gored front and a slightly gored back, and two v ide gores on either side. These gores require a whole width of the velveteen, and the wide slopes which are taken off are useful for trimming another gown. The skirt is lined throughout with jaconet, and the edge interlined with a five-inch strip of horse-hair. This latter adds greatly to the weight, and the new balayeuse, which is simply a ruche of ribbon or pinked silk, sets out the edge very nicely, particularly when full-flounced petticoats are worn as well. The back of the skirt is arranged in coarse gathers.

I am afraid the dress I am going to desoribe is somewhat beyond our means, but it is such a hand- some one I cannot help mentioning it. It is of silver-grey silk orepon made en Princesse, fasten- ing on the left side with paste buttons, and there is an Empire scarf of amber silk. A graceful apron of Tosca beads falls on the bodice. There are large rucked sleeves, draped and slashed with cream embroidered chiffon. These sleeves are somewhat elaborate, but they are extremely becom- ing. Yours lovingly,

WE PARTED AT THE ALTAR; 1

BY LAURA JEAN LIBBEY, Author of "A Mad Betrothal," "lone," "Parted by Fate," " Floribel's Lover," etc., etc.

CHAPTER XI. MRS. DORCHESTER'S COMPANION. LIKE one fascinated, the lady stood motionless ia the doorway, gazing at the rare beauty of the ' flower-like, girlish face before her. In a moment she recovered herself and stepped forward. "I am Mrs. Dorchester," she said, in a voice that was thoroughly refined and musical—" what can I do for you ? " "I am Miss Brandon," said Doris, timidly. "I have called from the registry office in response to your application for a governess." "A governess I" repeated the lady. "There has been a mistake. I want a companion." "I should like that quite as much as being a governess," said Doris, eagerly, "if I could only please you." Mrs. Dorchester looked at her anxiously. 'Do you know," she said—and Doris was struck with the bitterness in her voice—" that you have a very beautiful face?" "No one ever told me so before," said Doris, honestly. "Yet even were that the case, that fact could give me no pleasure." "No pleasure !' repeated the lady; and again Doris was struck by the exceeding bitterness of her voice, "Ah, if I had a face like yours, I would give the world and everything else in it; and yet you—to whom God has been so good—you do not prize it." "No," said Doris. "Then you are not vain," replied Mrs. Dor- chester, looking pleased. "If you came to me, I should want you to dress exceedingly plain— severely, strictly plain. I should want all that beautiful golden hair brushed tightly back from your face, and done up in a small knot at the back of your head. You would not like that." "It would not matter to me, madame, if you were pleased with me," replied Doris; and again the lady looked gratified. She was glad to be assured that the lovely young stranger did not care about being attractive. "Your duties would be very light," she persued. "I would want you to sit with me and talk to me. Sing to me that I could close my eyes and forget for a brief while. lam very lonely. I would want you to cheer me." "I would do my best," replied Doris, wondering greatly what manner of woman this was, who, surrounded by every luxury that the heart could desire, appeared so sad and lonely in all this splendour. As if reading her thoughts., the lady went on: "I do not care much for society. I have a horror of being courted and flattered for my wealth. I might have been happier had I been poor like you. My money will not buy me that which I crave above everything else in this world. If I had a beautiful face I might win my heart's desire," she went on, wistfully, tears filling her eyes, and her .bosom heaving convulsively. "If I had been offered my choice of all the gifts that Heaven gives to men wad women, I should have chosen beauty." Doris looted up into the plain homely face of the unhappy lady in wonder. If Doris had but known the pitiful story of her life, she could have under- stood that bitter longing. "I want some one near me who is very sym- pathetic," she said, earnestly. "You please me better than any one else whom they have sent from the agency. You will not complain at the stipulation. lam sure we can agree as to terms." "Oh, yes, madame. lam sure of that," responded Doris. Suddenly a thought seemed to flit through Mrs. Dorchester's brain. Rising hurriedly, she crossed to the mantel and took from it a cabinet photograph, which was inclosed in a closed velvet case. "'lbis is my husband's portrait,' she said, open- ing the case and handing it to Doris. "Although he married me—a plain woman—he is a great beauty-worshipper," she said, eyeing Doris keenly as sae spoke. One glance at the pictured face, and Doris turned pale and red by turns, almost falling to the floor in a deep swoon. The pictured face, smiling up at her from the crimson velvet frame, was the dark, handsome, wicked face of the strangtr who had accosted her on the agency steps. - Before Doris could utter the cry of astonishment and dismay that rose to her lips, there was the sound of approaching footsteps, the door was thrown open, and handsome, dashing Lawrence Dorchester, the merchant prince, stood on the threshold. Yes, there was no mistake. Her per- secutor of that morning was the original of the portrait she still held clutched in her trembling hand. His wife, watching him intently, noted the start of surprise and heard the exclamation that he crushed back as his eyes fell upon Doris. "This young lady, Miss Brandon, bas applied for the position of companion to me, Lawrence," explained Mrs. Dorchester. A perfect rapture of delight shone in his black

eyes. "She is admirably suited for it, I am sure— most admirably. You cannot do better than engage this very charming young lady at once. She will be a capital acquisition to our household, which has been dull beyond all endurance." No more embarrassing situation could be imagined. Mrs. Dorchester drew herself up with a proud, stately air. She grew white to the lips. It pained her to the very heart's core to have the secret sorrow of her life thus rudely laid base before this young stranger. "Ingage this pretty girl, by all means, my dear," lie said, turning to his wife with a low bow. "I am glad to see you are overcoming your pre- judice. My wife has always had a constitutional horror of pretty faces, as I have a constitutional liking ' for them," he said, with a laugh, turning to Doris. "I leave you to make your own arrangements. I shall be pleased to Bee you installed in my house.* With a very elaborate bow to' both, he quitted the room, smiling to Doris as he closed the door. "My husband does not mean anything by his flowery manner of speech," said Mrs. Dorchester, forcing a painful smile to her white, trembling lips. And Doris saw the effort she made to control her- self. Then, after a pause, the unhappy lady looked up at her. • I am very sorry," she said, "very sorry; but you are too young. I-1 need some one older than you. I cannot engage you." Doris understood perfectly why the unhappy lady had changed her mind so suddenly. She was but an innocent child, yet she was rapidly learning many of life s hard lessons; " I Am sorry." said Doris, gently. - You would-not have been in peace here," sighed the lady. " I—l—was mad, 1 think, not to have thought of this before. I am sorry I have detained you. Good morning, child. I wish you well." Doris quitted the grand mansion that held so much splendid misery, feeling as though one of the gates of life had been closed behind her. She must try again and.again ; it was useless to give up. She knew very well that she must find work or—starve. • She wondered vaguely, why the manageress of the agency had sent her to the very house of the man against whom she had warned her. It had been a mistake. While speaking of him the manageress had unintentionally jotted down that address. There was but one remaining place to go to, and, wearily enough, Doris wended her way there. Again Doris was ushered into a sumptuous -drawing-room, and the little serviog-maid an • pounced to the mistress, Mrs. Vane, that a pretty young wome* had been sent irom the agency this I time. "I hope she will suit," sighed Mrs. Vane, descending to the drawing-room She was quite pleased with Doris. "1 will see what my daughter thinks about engaging you for her younger sister. I usually consult her in everything. Send Miss Isabel here," she said to the servant who responded to the touch of the bell. "Dear me I what can she want," said the petulant voice in the upper corridor, and a moment later a young lady, with her hair still in curl papers, and a rather soiled blue silk wrapper, entered -the room. ," My dear," said the mother, humbly, "this young girl has come to apply for the position bs governess to your little sister, Daisy. Do you think she would suit us?" Isabel Vane stared haughtily at the lovely, flower-like face, and after a few remarks, rose and . quitted the room, calling her mother to join her for a moment. "You will muse me for a few moments: said

Mrs. Vane, anxious to make up in kindness tor tier Jacghter's lack of courtesy. Doris bowed. She soon heard the daughter in a high dispute with the mother in an adjoining room. "I really believe you have taken leave of your senses to even think of bringing a girl like that into the house for Captain Alden to see and fall in love with." "She seems like such a nice, modest young per- son, Isabel," said the mother—" really the nicest who has applied yet. She could be kept well in the background when the captain is here. I could manage that, I am sure." "If she knew there was a handsome young man coming to the house whom your daughter wished to marry, rest assured the artful minx would contrive to put herself in his way. No, no, she must not, she shall not come. I won't have her, I say. You ought to have known she wouldn't suit the moment you looked at her. A pretty governess in the house to try to outwit me with my lover! I say it isn't to be thought of! lie's coming to call for me this forenoon to go riding in the park; get her out of the house before he comes," she cried, shrilly. Isabel Vane flounced upstairs, and her mother returned to Doris, never dreaming she had heard all. "I am sorry. You are a little too inexperienced, I fear," she said, kindly. "I am sorry, but I can- not engage you." Tears sprang to Doris's pretty blue eyes, and that quite distressed the gentle, tender-hearted lady. She arose quickly to depart. "Stay a moment, my good giil," said Mrs. Vane, laying a kindly hand on her arm. " Perhaps I can put you in the way of obtaining a good position. Now that I think of it, I remember Mrs. Thornton, the banker's wife, wants a companion. I will give you a note to her." The words had hardly left her lips ere Doris threw up her hands, and fell in a dead faint upon the floor.

CrumEn XIL IN THE SERVANTS' HALL. WHEN Doris opened her eyes, she found Mrs. Vane bending over her in the greatest consterna- tion. Out in the corridor Miss Isabel's voice could be heard, shrilly giving orders that the girl must be taken from the drawing-room down to the ser- vants' hall, without delay. "It will not be necessary," said Doris, strug- gling to her feet. "I am going now, madame." " Was it the closeness of the room that made you faint, my poor child? or—or did you faint from want of—of food, or anything of that kind? Pardon me, I could not send you away so, if it was," said Mrs. Vane, pitying the sweet, death- white face. Doris flushed. "It was only a sudden faintness that seized me, madame—a sharp pain at my heart," she answered, truthfully; but she did not add that it was the mention of her handsome, faithless young hus- band's mother's name that brought that terrible pain there. Frederick's mother wanted a companion. Dare she—the forsaken bride, the injured young wife— go to her? The very thought almost took her breath away, and bewildered her. Her head was in a whirl; her heart beat so loud and so fast, she was sure the lady must hear it. "I hope you will be successful, my child," said Mrs. Vane, bowing her out of the drawing-room. "Too pretty to suit," whispered the maid to Doris as she led the way to the front door. "If you went to Lamartine, and got him to paint a few wrinkles on your face, you would not rind it half so hard to get a pl ice." "Who is Larnartine ? " asked Doris, scarcely knowing herself why she asked the question. He is the man to whom every one in New York goes for disguises," laughed the maid. "He can change any one so completely that one can hardly recognise one's self. Why, a forger who had taken a great amount of money lately, stayed near the scene of the robbery for weeks. He was so cleverly disguised, his own brother did not know him. When the affair leaked out, it was found he had been to Laniar tine." Doris's every nerve thrilled as she listened. A sudden idea flashed through her brain, Why should she not go to Lamartine? If he could disguise her in perfect safety, she would dare to enter her husband's home. Different characters yield to different tempta- tions. The temptation to enter the home of Frederick's mother—look upon the face of the husband who had so ruthlessly deserted her, almost at the very altar,—was so sweet, so subtle, she could not resist it, for, Heaven help her, in spite of all, she loved her handsome young hus- band still. "Loved him with a bitter yearning that would never pass away; Loved him with a ceaseless passion that would never know decay." Yes, she would go to Lamartine. It must have been fate that caused the maid at Mrs. Vane's to utter those words to her. She found the place with but little difficulty. It was noon hour, and Monsieur's parlours were for the time deserted. Doris entered timidly, and a moment later the dapper little "artist," as he chose to call himself, appeared. "You wish to see me, madame ?" he said. "In what can I be of service to you ?" "1 want—a disguise," she answered, "so com- plete—so perfect—l could not be recognised by anyone who ever saw me before." "It will be difficult to find one like that," he said, looking at the lovely young face in intense surprise. 'A mystery, a romance; perhaps an elopement will be the outcome Of this affair," he thought. "I see a way to meet your wishes, madame," he said aloud, " but it will be a sad pity to change such a face—for any reason; still, if you desire it to be done, I shall comply, of course." "I want it done," said Doris. He gave her a hand basin containing a dark, clear liquid and a sponge, and bade her sponge her face lightly with the mixture; and thougn she had come there purposely for the disguise, now that she was on the point of acquiring it, her whole soul rose in rebellion against it. Monsieur Larnartine saw her hesitation, and fancied that she was anxious about her clear, white delicate skin. "You? need not fear it," he said; "the liquid is quite harmless. All trace of it will disappear if you use this," showing her a bottle of clear amber liquid. "Three drops of this, put into cold water will, at any time, remove the stain. Bathe your face well with the sponge." She obeyed him shrinkingly. Then, indicating another seat before a table covered with toilet articles and innumerable labelled bottles, he bade her unbind her lovely golten curls. For quite half an hour he busied himself over the long, shining tresses. Then he asked her to look in the glass. Doris looked, and at staring in wonder at the strange face the mirror reflected. Silo would never have recognised her own self. She saw a d -rk-brown face, decked arched eye- brows and lashes, a low lorhead half revealed, half concealed by a mass of dark, wavy hair. "If those blue eyes were but black you would be a perfect brunette, and the change in your appear- ance complete. Are you satisfied?" Doris bowed her head in assent. NI, lien she paid Monsieur Lamartine his charges, she found she had • not one cent left. "I hope whatever object this has to serve is an honourable one, and may therefore help your purpose," he said, bowing Doris out of the parlour. Out on the street Doris shrank from the gaze of passers-by. It almost seemed to her they must know she was not what she represented herself to , be. Though she had adopted this measure for looking again upon her husband's face, hearing his voice, remaining unrecognised herself, there was romethino so false and deceptive about it that her meet, candid nature rebelled against it; yet the one passionate, craving desire to enter the home 0 the husband who had deserted her led her on. By patient inquiry Doris found that the home of the Thorntons was in the suburbs of the metropolis. She was obliged to walk the distance. At length she reached the villa. Ah what a magnificent place it was !—on the brow of the hill that sloped gently to the glittering Hudson. Her heart gave a great bound. This was Frederick's home ! Timidly she entered the great arched gateway and walked up the long, paved walk to the house. Green, velvety, close-shaven lawns, brilliant with beds of rare flowers, dotted here and there, stretched out on either side. Marble statues gleamed amid the green foliage, and fountains rippled in the sunshine. Gorgeous peacocks strutted over the greensward, and song•lrirris swung \Ally to and fro in their gilded cages on the serandas of the grand grey stone house. "Ah me l" sobbed Doris. bitterly, "was thers:

ever snort a bitter fate as mine? I should be coining here to-day as Frederick's happy bride, instead of creeping up to these doors, apply- ing for the position of paid companion to hill haughty lady mother." Suddenly from around the corner of the building sprang a ferocious dog, and at his heels was a tall slip of a girl, in a fluttering white dress, her red, tangled hair flying in the breeze, her cheeks glow- ing, and her eyes bright with the excitement. Doris's heart beat fast. Intuitively, she knew this must be Frederick's youngest sister—" Trixy, the Romp," as he had designated her. Before she could take but that first fleeting glance, the dog had observed the stranger, and sprang toward her with a loud, savage growl. Doris recoiled in terror, uttering a piteous cry of dismay. "Down, Tiger, down," cried Trixy Thornton, seizing the dog by the collar as she gained his side quite out of breath. "For shame, frightening a stranger so." "He won't harm you," she said, turning to Doris. "He looks big and ferocious, but he's only an overgrown puppy. Who did you wish to see?" she asked, coming ciuser to Doris, and looking curiously into the sweet, sad, dark face. "Mrs. Thornton," replied Doris. "I have brought a letter from Mrs. Vane. I have come to apply for the pesitioit of companion to Mrs. Thorn- ton." "My gracious ! you'd make a much better companion for me," laughed Trixy, shaking back her inane of tangled red curls, and whistling for the dog to follow. "Come this way, please. You shall see mamma at once.. I hope she will engage you." Doris's heart warmed to the impulsive girl at once. She could have knelt down beside her, and kissed her pretty, white hands. Trixy led Doris directly to her mother's boudoir. "Mamma," she said, "here is a young lady who brings you a letter from Mrs. Vane." Mrs. Thornton glanced up languidly from her book, and motioning Doris to a seat, carefully perused the letter. "Mrs. Vane has omitted mentioning your name: May I inquire what it is ? " she asked at

- length. "Doris Carlisle," replied Doris, faintly, and she shuddered, recalling the old trite' and true lines: _ _ .

Oh, what a tangled web we weave When first we practice to deceive." No one ever yet practised a deception hut what she found herself entangled in many more to escape the consequences of the first. It was so with poor Doris. "You are quite young, Miss Carlisle," replied Mrs. Thornton, turning a pair of dark eyes on her, that reminded her so much (.f Frederick's. " Still, you could have no better reaommendation than letter from Mrs. Vane. Dear me, Miss Carlisle, what are you gazing at so intently?" It was a portrait in crayon of Frederick Thorn- ton. Doris was gazing at the pictured, smiling face with her heart in her eyes. Mrs. Thornton's remark, and the haughty wonder in her voice, recalled Doris's scattered senses. " The portrait is—is--so like your face, madame," stammered Doris, confusedly. "It is the portrait of my son," replied Mrs. Thornton, the frown clearing from her face once more. She was always pleased when any one spoke of a resemblance between her handsome son and herself. This proud, cold woman loved her daughters after her own stately fashion, but she fairly idolised her son. To Doris's intense delight, after answering the usual questions, Mrs. Thornton decided to engage her as companion. "When can you come, Miss Carlisle ?" she asked. "At once," replied Doris, fearing to leave her lest she should change her mind. "That will please me," said Mrs. Thornton- " Your luggage can be sent for to-morrow." Thus it was that poor Doris, the deserted bride, gained an entrance into the household of her mother-in-law. Only Heaven could have foretold where this rash step was to lead. It would have been better for Doris had death claimed her then vid there.

WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP

CANAL.

INTERVIEW WITH THE CHAIRMAN OF

THE RIVERS' COMMITTEE.

Another Conference was held at the Warrington Town Hall on Monday last week with reference to the subject in dispute between the Corporation and the Ship Canal Company. There were present on be- half of the Corporation the following members of the Rivers' Committee :—Councillor Henry Roberts (chairman), Councillor Robert Heaton (deputy- chairman), Aldermen W. Bolton, T. Hutchinson, and T, Sutton ; Councillors A. Bennett, L. Greening, F. W. Monks, and William Pierpoint (Alderman Fran- Comb being the only member of the committee absent.) Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk) and Mr. T. Longdin (borough surveyor) were also present. The Manchester Ship Canal Company were repre- sented by Mr. Bythell (manager), Mr. Southern, and another Manchester director ; Sir Leader Wil- limos, Mr. Hunter (engineer), Mr. Collier (of the Bridgewater Trust), Mr. Marshall Stevens, and Mr. Whitworth (the secretary of the company.) The Proceedings at the Conference were conducted Privately no reporters being admitted, but later in the week a representative of the "Examiner" called nPon Mr. Roberts who since he became chairman of the Rivers' Committee has championed the interest of the Corporation and the town generally with great tact and ability. To the question as to whether the Conference bad led to a settlement of any of the questions in dispute Mr. Roberts replied with a decided negative. The two or three hours of ani- mated discussion had evidently not brought the Corporation and the Canal Company much, if any, nearer a mutual agreement. To a question as to whether the Rivers' Commit- tee had made concessions to the Canal Company as they had been charged with doing in some quarters. Mr. Roberts replied, " Well the only concessions Made by the Rivers' Committee, as has been stated rnore than once, have been purely time ' conces-1 Sion is 8 ; that is, a longer time has been allowed for ""e Performing of certain work beyond what was atated in the Act of Parliament. Further, they were to pay Penalty of .£5O a day for every day, after the time 41ireed upon, that the channel remained undredged. GREAT SANKEY PARISH COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of the Great Sankey Parish Council was held in the Board schools, on qonday even- ing. The chair was occupied.by Mr. W. P. Skelton,and there were also present : Messrs. H. Bate. G.'Phompson, W. H. L. Gorse, T. Halton, T. Kilshaw, Fairclough, and J. Wright (clerk). CONTROL OF HIGHWAYS. A letter was read from Mr. W. El. Poole, chairman of the Deane (Bolton) Parish Council,stating that very great dissatisfaction existed in that district, because the con- trol of the parish highways for local matters had been taken from under local control. Several parish meetings had been held and resolutions passed unanimoasly, to bring these roads lack under local management. They desired the Great Sankey l'arish Council to pass a simi- lar resolution to be sent to the Local Government Board, the member tor the division, and the C-ainty Council.— The Chairman expressed himself in favour of the pro- posal, as he said that it was no use of them meeting together unless there was something to do. He thought the Council should have control over local highways and footpaths of which there was a considerahle number in that district. It seemed to be the feeling throughout the country that Parish Councils were simply bodies of gentlemen called together with practically nothing to do.—Mr. Thompson was of a similar opinion as the Chairman with regard to the high ways.—Mr. Bate said that the Highway Board was still in existence, but Mr. Wright said that it would only exist another three years. —The Chairman said that it had been decided that when the management of the roads passed out of the hands of the Highway Board it would be given to the District Committee,and not to the Parish Committee. The object o,f the resolution was to prevent that being done.—lt was ultimately decided to pass a resolution in terms similar to that suggested by the Deane Parish Cuuncil.

TILE TREASURERSHIP. The Chairman said thtt Mr. Murray, of Parr's Bulk had accepted the position of treasurer, awl he had filled up a guarantee form for £2OO. The Parish Council wc uld have to pay the premium of the policy.

APPOINTMENT OF OVERSEERS. The Chairman said that it was the duty of the Parish Council te appoint the overseers and assistant overseers, and as the term of office of the present overseers expired during the present month, it would he necessary to have a special meeting for the purpose of appointing their successers.—lt was decided on the motion of Mr. Thomp- son, seconded by Mr. Fair,lough that the meeting be held on Monday next at 7.30 p.m. THE PARISH CHEST. The Chairman said that as all the members of the Council would observe,the parish chest was in the room. As they would remember, a resolution was passed at the last meeting that Mr. Bate and himself, as overseers, should call upon the proprietor of the Cnapel House to make arrangements for the transferring of the cheat. Mr. Bate had, however been unable owing to sickness, to do so. In the meantime, he (Mr. Skelton) had received the following interesting account : "To the overseers of the township of Great Sankey. to John Warburton, March, 1895, to 38 years of storage of the old parish chest, at Sc. per year, £9 10s." That was received by him with a considerable amount of amusement. (Laughter.) The bill was also accompanied by the following letter from Mr. C. H. Unsworth, solicitor. 11 Lyrne-Areet. War- rington :—" Dear sire—l am instructed by Mr. Warbur- ton to forward the enclosed account, on payment of 'which you can remove the parish chest." (Laughter.) The letter also expressed disapprobation of cartai nmem- hers of the Council by having opened the chest, and stated that in so doing they were undoubtedly guilty of trespass. Re considered that there was no right for the claim of storage, as Mr. Warburton had never given notice of claiming such right previously. The bill had caused eonsiderable amusement among his Warrington friends. With regard to the statement that some mem- bers of the Parish Council had been guilty of trespass, he (Mr. Skelton) might say that he went to examine the chest, and Mrs. Unsworth, the landlady of the Chapel House, not only supplied them with the key, but also with a candle, and even held the candle while they examined the chest. (Laughter.) He therefore failed to see where the trespass came in. He had, however, since received a letter saving that the Council could have the chest, and there it was —The chest was then opened, and a large number of documents were exam- ined. They proved to be chiefly bills, L.—, in connec- tion withthe poor relief of Great Sankey, some of them datinc as far ago as 1784.

At the Liverpool Assign on Tuesday,James Wagstaffe collier, was acquitted of the charge of having murdered his stepson William Henry Isiolmes, a lad of eleven years or age, at Tyldesley, on December 2, by throwing him down a ,dieused pit shaft.

lilt. L T. BRUNNER, 31P, AND WELSH

DISESTABLISEIMENT.

L PUNGENT LETTER.

The members of the Northwich Constitutional Club recently forwarded to Mr. 1. T. Brunner, M.P., a resolu- tion passed at a public meeting of the club tc the fol- lowing effect :—•• That as the bill for diseltahlishing and dinendowing the four Welsh dioceses of the Church of England is, in the opinion of this meeting,bad in prin- ciple, and a bill which will inflict serious injury upon the poorer portion of the population, the electors are hereby called upon to resist t e measure by all the legiti- mate means in their power.' To this Mr. Brunner has forwarded the following reply::—" Dear Sir,—our favour of the iBth inst. came to hand yesterday mornine. ?have read the resolution enclosed with care, and I beg to assure you with respect also. Though I cannot agree with the opinions expressed in the resolution, lam glad to note that the members of your club hold them- selves bound to consider the interest of the poorer part of the population of Wales, and to paint out that the till makes large provision tor their interest by way of hos- pitals and trained nurses for the sick poor, improved dwellings, and so on. I thing it extremely likely that before long a compromise will be affected on the lines of Mr. Chamberlain's letter to the Welsh editor, the Tory party throwing over some of their principles to secure some of the funds, and the Liberal 'party giving way on the question of money for the sake of their principles. You describe the meeting at the Constitutional Club at which the resolution was passed as a public meeting The word public' ig to be taken in a Pickwickian sense. I take it.—Yours faithfully, JonN T. -- R fiCTNNHI2." •

The Swintonians were dumbfounded, it was so neatly done. Burton failed to improve the point. This was about seven minutes from the close of the game, and during the whole of that time Warrington kept the upperhand, and never allowed their opponents to become dangerous, and when the whistle sounded, Warrington had won a most unexpected, but at the same time well-earned, and well-deserved victory by a I try to nil. WARRINGTON A v. SWINTON A.—The Warrington A team stayed at home on Saturday last, to face the Swinton A team. The afternoon was fine and a large number of on-lookers gathered to witness the encounter between these two well-known teams. When lined up on the field the teams were composed as tollows :—War- rington Mannion, full back; Sankey, Forster, Lawless, Evans, three-quarter backs; Carey, Wood, half backs ; Barratt, Burrows, Bandies, Swift, Andrewa, Hill, Makin and Hughes. forwards. Swinton : Berry, full back ; Gad, Halley, Glover, Sharpies, three-quartr backs ; Smith, Booth, half-backs; Jackson (captain), Berry, Gully, Williams Spruce, Tongue, Hayes and Buckley, forwards. Swinton kicked off punctually at 3 30, the advertised time, towards the pavilion goal, Warrington returned with great speed and seeing it was of no use Hughes kicked the ball dead. Warrington began their pressing mode taking the leather in the visitors' terrhory. Swinton seeing their task was a heavy one, succeeded in preventing their opponents in scoring. A minute later Lawless secured and tumbling over one of the Swinton's three-quarter backs, scored the first try for Warrington amid loud cheers. Carey kicked for goal, but this player was unsuccessful, his kick falling short. The visitors regained their footing, they taking the ball to the homesters' quarters, a scrimmage result- ing near the goal hue. The ball was then taken towards the railway goal. Here Makin made a good attempt at scoring but was too slow, he being overt) swered by one of his opponents. The ball was again kicked up by Warrington and this time Hill added to the scoring list. Forster attempted to ' Improve the score by kicking for goal, but the attempt was futile. Warrington still kept the best of the game until half-time when the score read Warrington A 2 tries Swinton nothing. Warrington resumed and Swinton savagely attacked, and Sharpies succeeded in putting the ball over the goal line. Play was now at a stand still for a few seconds one of the Swinton men being hurt, but it was soon resumed, he having re- covered from his injuries. A number of scrimmages and lice-outs took place on the visitors' goal track, the Swinton men playing hard to keep their opponents from scoring. At length the leather was takee to the homes- ters' territory where the play became rough on both sides. Warrington still kept up their pressure and Woods (Warrington's new half back) secured, and passed to Carey and this player ran and overpowering his an- tagonist placed the ball over the goal line. Lawless neatly converting. Play was of a mild nature up to final time. The score then read Warrington 1 goal 2 tries, Swinton 1 try. WARRINGTON RANGNRS v. LONDON AND NORM-WES- TERN Loco's.—This match took place on the ground of the latter. The Loco's. won the toss, and played down hill. RUG BY.

WARRINGTON V. SWINTON.

The Warrington team have been doing so badly this year, that their supporters have become quite down- hearted. Since the advent of the present year and pre- vious to last Saturday, Alley bara not won a single match, losing two on their own ground- Consequently when they were due at swinton—a place where they had never scored a solitary victory—every one but the most sanguine were prepared for another downfall. Swint m had on the previous Saturday beaten Rochdale Hornets by three tries to nil, and, as the Hornets on the follow- ing Tuesday had beaten Warrington by a try to nil the I Swinton team ought to beat Warrington easily by all c&culations of form. Football is, however, very uncer- tain, and conurary to all expectations, Warrington were able to score their first victory on the Swinton ground on Saturday. Splendid weather favoured the match, from a spectator's point of view, though it was rather warm tor Lhe players. The Swintonians have always been known up to the present as the" Lions," owing to the headquarters being at the White Lion. They have, however, recently removed to the Bull's Head, a place which is not nearly so convenient,as it is such a long dis- tance from the field. The name of " Lions "is probably too firmly fixed upon them now, or we might hear them termed Swinton" Bulls." Mr. Higgins, of Manchester, acted as referee at the match on Saturday, and the two neutral touch judges were Mr. R. H. Carson, of Man- chester, and Mr. T. Melledew, ot Rochdale Hornets. The following were the two teams : Warrington g Boscow, full back ; Barber, Heesam, Moores, and Burton, threequarters ; John Bate and Houghton, half backs ; Turner, Taylor, Dakin, Berry, Saunders, Callaghan, Bailey,and Thorniley, forwards. Swinton : Winterbottom, full hick ; Valentine, Pear- son Tyldesley, and Lewis, threequarters ; Brockbank and Harris, half backs ; Sharpies, Knowles, Harris, Cleminson, Tickle, Murray, Hallam, and Evans, for- wards. There were between 4,000 and 5,000 spectators pre- sent when Turner kicked off,Warrington having the sun in their eyes during the first half. Pearson returned Turner's kick to half way, and Warrington rushed the ensuing scrimmage in fine style,Winterbottom stopping them when they were getting dangerous. Play settled down for a time in the Swinton 25, and Burtan almost forced his way in, Tyldesley and Lewis tackling at a critical moment. Swinton were at length awarded a free kick,wnich was taken by Valentine, but Thorniley charginr, it down was just prevented from scoring by Lewis. Up to new Swinton were clearly ovarplayed,but Cleminaon started a dribble on his own account which was brought to an end by Burtoe,at halfway. Swinton profiting by this relief pressed still further, and on one occasion all the Warrington team were passed but Bos- cow. The full back was, however, equal to the emer- gency, and prevented what seemed to be a certain score for Swinton. Barber now got posses- sion, and his ran along touch was an eye-opener to the spectators. He was not floored by Valentine until he had put his side on the offensive. Hallam now broke away., but Moores accounted for him, and Warrington attacked so strongly that a score seemed imminent every moment. Valentine at length got to the half way line, when he found a stumbling block in I Boscow. Disaster at this stage almost befel War- rington, for Turner taking a free kick unaccountably sent the ball towards his own goal, and Pearson racing up, but for Boscow, would have had a clear field to get in. Boscow, however, just spoiled him. This attack of Swinton was only short, and Heesom kicked across to Burton. The latter just failed to field the ball, other- wise he could have walked in with no one to stop him. Following this let off, a free kick was given against Swinton for infringement of the rules. Burton's shat at goal did nat come Off., and a minor was the only result. Heesont replied to Valentine's kick out and found touch within a yard of the line, but Swinton staved off the danger. An unpleasant incident here occurred. Pear- son dallying with the ball in his possession, Fleesom rushed forward to tackle. Heesom reached him just after he had parted with the ball, and the two came to the ground. Pearson was winded, and the game was stopped . for a short time. The Swinton spectators gave vent to their feeling in angry tones. The incident was, however, merely a misjudgment on Fleesom's part, Pearson having dallied too long with the ball, and the former thinking that he had the ball /still in his possession. Pearson soon recovered and the game was resumed, Warrington attacked hotly, and Houghton was knocked into touch on the line. A slight relief was experienced by Tickle breaking away from the subsequent line out, but Moores scoring passed to Barber, who was knocked into touch just in the same fashion as Houghton. Swinton took good advantage of these two narrow escapes, for over- coming almost all opposition. Valentine would almost certainly have scored but for Barber. This was only a spurt on the part of Swintoe, for Warrington again returned to the attack,, and were causing the Swlatonisas great anxiety when toe interval arrived with a clean sheet. Following Valentine's kick off and Boscow's return, even play was the rule for some time after the restart. Much amusement was caused by Barber, who, finding that he could not stop Valentine any other way, stuck like a vice to his foot, and dispite the "big one's" efforts to get free, he held him there until he could be securely tackled. Swint= now began to have the upper hand, and a fierce struggle took ,place on the Warrington line. This was brought to an end by Berry, Callaghan and Saunders breaking away and dribbling to the quarter flag. but Swinton again returned to the attack and made desperate attempts to score, Valentine in particu- lar. Warrington defended as ch sperately. and Taylor at length broke away from his own goal line and from his kick, Berry tackled Winterbottom at the quarter flag. This was a great relief to Warrington, and from now to the end of the game, the play was much more even. The Swinton forwards were again bearing down on the Warrington goal. when Burton made a daring save by kic,ring the ball over their heads in the associa- tion style and finding touch. Warrington now got the upper hand and the home defenders found plenty of work to do. Turner put some good work in for War- ring, and Valentine in his repeated attempts to break away invariably found his way blocked either by , Barber, Moorea or Callaghan. Swinton by means of

PARR V. RUNCORN.—At Parr, on Saturday, in glorious weather, to replay their tie in the above competition. THE SEW PUBLIC HALL.

On Friday afternoon:last week a sub-committee of the General Purposes Committee of the Warrington Corporation waited upon Mr. J. C. Parr to confer with him with regard to the naming of the new public hall which has been presented to the town by Mr. Parr and which will soon be ready for opening. It was suggested by the sub-committee that the new hall should be called °The Parr Hall," and as Mr. Parr agreed with this proposal that is the name by which it will be known after its opening. Mr. Parr's opinion was also invited as to the form the opening ceremony should take, and he expressed a preference for the performance of an oratorio. The hall will, therefore, be opened with a musical performance of this character under the auspices of the Corporation. It is expected that the Warrington Musical Society will be asked to take a leading part in arranging the performance, the Corporation finding the principal vocalists and any necessary augmentation of the band.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

FOOTBALL.

Since the present y;;;1;-e-g-a-n, there has not been much good news in the football line in Warrington, but, now as everyone knows, the spell of misfortune has been broken, and Warrington have at length won a victory, and that at Swinton of all places. They not only won, but fairly earned their victory, for they did not win by a fluke. The victory was won by downright hard work, and now that Warrington have COMA Out Of their shell, they ought to keep the ball rolling. If they do,it will not be the first time they have "come strong " at the close of the football season.

It is a very singular record which exists between the Warrington and Swinton clubs, and which is now broken. Previous to Saturday, Warrington had never won a single match at Swinton, nor Swinton one at Warrington. There had been two draws between the clubs, and singular to say, one was at Warrington, and the other at Swinton. It is a very peculiar circumstance that Warrington should boat Swinton for the first time away, when they have been doing so badly of late. They have had all the best of the matches with tho " Lions" during the present and preceeding season, for out of four matches played, they have won three and drawn one. In fact, the lest time Swinton best War- rington was in the season of 1892-3 Out of the eight points which Warringtan have gained in the league, half of them have been taken at the expense of Swinton, and the solitary goal which they have gained in the competition was also gained against Swinton, so that Warrington have much to thank this famous club for.

It was with anything but confidence that the wire- pullers jcurneyed to Swinton on Saturday, for had not their opponents beaten their conquerors on the previous Tuesday? They, however, played for all they were worth ,and thus gained a most unexpected and welcome victory. The few Warringtonians who were present among the spectators did not forget to let the colliers know that they were there, especially when Barber ran in the winning try. In fact, the strength of their lungs rather astonished the natives. The Swinton spectators are a very demonstrative lot, and woe-betide any un- fortunate visiting player if he does anything which they do not consider quite above board.

The Warrington team on Saturday was a very well balanced one. The forwards were superior to the Swin- ton eight, and there was not a " passenger " among them. It would be invidious to single any one out for special mention. As there is, however, at present so much prejudice in Warrington against new players, for a very cogent reason, it may be here mentioned that Warrington have an acquisition in Callaghan, who has now played for two or three weeks for them. Ile can take the position at three-quarter with great credit when it is required.

" Jack " Bate was at his old post at half hack, owing to the indisposition of "Smack." and with Houghton he appeared quite at home. It was his pass to Barber, which culminated in the latter scoring the try. The trial of Floughton at half back has been a success. Bar- ber was more than a match for Valentine to whom he was opposed, and he really excelled himself on Saturday. His try was a beauty, and Valentine was considerably astonished at the way he had been tricked. The other threequarters also played a very good came, and two of them,llleesom and' Burton, materially helped towards getting the try. Boscow was very safe, and he ought surely to have been tried again f)r the county. It would not be too much to say that he has scarcely a superior in Lancashire. The way he has recovered his form is wonderful.

It will be noticed by the League table that Warring- ton have again regained the fourth position. They ought to keep that, even if they do not go higher up to the end of the season. There is a chance of getting third as they did last year, and a remote one of getting second. They ought surely therefore to finish fourth at the least. Below will be seen the result of the competition up to date :

For. Agst. Pld. Won. Lost. Drn. G.T. G.T.Pts. Teich.,ley Oldham St. Helens Barrington Swinton Rochdale Hornets Broughton Rangers Leigh (on September 19), Salford (October 1f). and Wigan (November 13),having been suspended for professionalism, will not take any tart in the competition this season.

To-day Warrington meet Leigh, and on Monday they meet the Oldham team. They have an excellent chance of winning to-day, but how will they fare on Monday, against the team which made such an exhibition of them at the beginning of the season ? One thing lam almost certain of, and that is that Oldham will not run up a score of 29 points to three as they did then. If the ball is kept pretty close,Warricgton will even stand a chance of winning. However, it is easier to " prophecy after the event." The following is the team selected against Leigh and Oldham :—Boscow,full back ; Barber,Moerts. Heesorn and Burton, three-quarters ;John T. Bate, and James Bate, half-backs • Turner, Dakin, Taylor, B• rry, Thornley, Houghton, Callaghan and Bailey, forwards.

The A team also gained a victory over the winton A on Saturday, and that by a goal and two tries to a try. On only one occasion, and that during the present sedson, have the Swinton A lowered the colours of War- rinton, and therefore the result ix not a very surprising one. Warrington had a new half back, Wood, and he shaped very well.

CYCLING.

As we announced last week Saturday was the day chosen for the opening club run of the season, and in the event of wet weather supervening a 'bus waa to he pro- vided. Fortunately the last resort" was rendered UTl- necessary by the typical cycling weather which held sway. Cronton was the destination, and at the trait from tire" Norton Arms' the club made a very fine show, the members having turned up in large numbeis with a quantity of new machines. On reaching 13,dd a contingent of the Widnes CC. joined the [tame club and a D afternoon of thorough eujoyment was experiercel. On reaching Cronton a game of football was organised. commenced and carried out with great zest and enirtv, ment, except on the part of one player whose anthu- siasm carried him into the ditch. Mr. S. Baldly. one of the cyclists, who had come armed with a camera, thPn photographed the members and friends present, while another photograph which will provide amusement for some time to come, was taken by the same gent leman of bliose members whose spirits were too exuberant,ard to curtail which their friends pinioned them in the stocks, in which position of "dignity and ease they were , "snapped." While en route Mr. Baldry " snaoshotted " the cyclists. An excellent tea was provided by Host I Houghton with hie customary iclat, .after which the I usual" smoker " was held.

COLLISION BETWEEN STEAMERS.

On Saturday ink.rning a collision attended with some- what serious consequences occured on the Ship Canal, in Ince cutting, between Saltport and Etstliam,betweem the steamers Blackrock and Mersey. The former was loaded with coal, and was proceeding seawards, whilst the latter was eA route for Partington, to take in cargo. THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, MARCH 23 1895.

Tim dispute in the shoe trade Lock-out in the Shoe Trade, has reached very large propor- tions. There are now, it is said, no fewer than two hundred thousand workpeople in this trade alone who are prevented from follow- ing their usual avocations and- earning their weekly wages by the " lock-out " which has been decreed by the Federation of Manufacturers. In the face of an announcement like this the first question which naturally arises in one's mind is, What is the cause of such a disastrous dislocation of trade? The question is, however, easier asked than answered. The dispute has not been brought about by any single or simple cause, such as a demand for an increase or a reduction of wages, or for longer or shorter;hours of labour. The shoe trade has been passing through a transition stage. Twenty or thirty years ago almost the whOle of the processes of making shoes were carried out by hand labour, but one machine has been intro- duced after another until now it appears that a well-equipped shoe factory is quite an arsenal of the most ingenious mechanism. In 1859 there was a long and bitter strike against the introduction - of sewing machines, but the workmen were beaten on that occasion, and it would seem that since then they have shown no great aversion to the use of im- proved labour saving appliances. As a matter of fact•they have had little reason to do so, as the trade has increased by "leaps and bounds," and has become one of the most prosperous in the country. We are told that while the hosiery trade in the neighbourhood of Leicester has io recent years steadily declined that of shoe- making on a wholesale scale has just as steadily improved, with the result that those who were formerly makers of hosiery now earn a living by undertaking one or other of the numerous separate processes by which shoes are made. In other respects, besides that of changing from a hand in- dustry to a machine industry, the shoe trade has been passing through a transition period. A quarter of a century or more ago, shoe-making, being a handicraft, was carried on for the most Part at the workman's own home, but with the advent of machinery that method of working has been and is still being gradually superseded by the more effective fac- tory system. It seems, therefore, that the differ- ences which have led to the present iock-out have been caused by the re-arrangements which were inevitable in transforming a large trade from a hand industry to a machine industry and in es- tablishing factory labour for borne work. So far as outsiders can judge this seems to be in a pre- eminent degree a dispute ix which the goodtffices of competent and impartial arbitrators should be invited. Unfortunately, however, the employers have so far positively declined to have anything to do with arbitration. It is riot easy to divine what their motives are for taking up this uncom- promising attitude. It is true, they allege that in previ9ui instances, in smaller disputes, the workmen have not always abided by the decision of arbitrators, but then, on the other hand, simi- lar charges are made against the masters by the workmen. In any case this seems but a poor pre- tence for subjecting hundreds of thousands of people to the dire destitution which will result from a prolonged strike. If arbitration were adopted on the present occasion it would be little less than a national affair, and public opiceion would almost as a matter of course be so strongly with the arbitrators that neither side could afford to

defy their decision. The President of the Federa- tion of manufacturers is reported to have said that when the workmen's union " have spent all their money they will not be able to run away from the conditions imposed upon them." If this pro- nouncement indicates the spirit in which the manufacturers have entered upon the conflict,there need be no surprise at their rejection of arbitra- tion. Arbitration might find a settlement of the grievances as to methods of work in the shoe trade, but it would not cause the workmen "° spend all their money." That is a different object altogether, as it really means the beating down of the trade union of the operatives.

Taxitz have been few more pithy Church Defence. and pungent letters written On

political subjects than that in which Mr. J. T. BaurrNmn, M.P., replies to the members of the Northwich Constitutional Club. The resolution which the " Constitutionalists "of Northwich forwarded to Mr. BRUNNER on" demned, as a matter of course, the Welsh Dia- establishment Bill, and, singular to say, one of the grounds of condemnation is that the measure will "inflict serious injury upon the poorer por- tion of the population." Mr. BRUNNER takes note of this remarkable allegation, and points out that the Bill makes large provisions for the interest of the poor by way of hospitals and trained nurses for the sick poor, improved dwellings, and so 00. And then he adds for the consolation of his "con- stitutional" constituents, "I think it extremell likely that before long a compromise will be effected on the lines of Mr. CILAMIIERLAIN'S letter to the Welsh editor, the Tory party throwing °Ter some of their principles to secure some of the funds, and the Liberal party giving way on the question of money for the sake of their principles? We do not cnow whether the members of the Constitutional Club will feel perfectly happy with this reply from Mr. BRUNNER, but they ought to do. If Mr. BRUNNER'S prediction be fulfilled, and we think it will, they will have the satisfaction ef knowing that their brother Constitutionalists in Parliament have succeeded in retaining for the Welsh clergy a large proportion of the "loaves and fishes," and at mil an easy price too—simply that of "throwing over some of their principles? An e3sier feat than that for modern " Constientionae lists" it Wetild be difficult to imagine. The defen- ders of the Welsh Church are, taking one thing with another, having a bad time of it just nolv• An eminent Churchman, Canon FREMANTLE, has within the last few days written a cogent and powerful protest against the methods of Chuln'''. defence that have been adopted. He declares that the movement is far too clerical to produce any conviction, and that it cannot but appear to the nation as an effort of the clergy to retain their position and privileges. He adds what has been often said before but which comes with ail the greater force from 'a Church clergyman, that the policy of Church defence is dragging tlle clergy down to the lower regions of politics, and that it associates them with a part 9 which has been the defender of class privi- leges. It would seem from what he says that matters have gone so far that churchmen are actually asked to pray for the temporalities of the Welsh clergy against the Liberal party. And then, unkindest cut of all, Canon FREMANTLE actuallY speaks disparagingly of the precious lectures On Church history which have beengiven so frequentlY of late. He declares that the history that i 5 dispensed by Church defenders is not true history at all, and only gives occasions for crushing rejoinders. This must surely be very discourag" ing to the youthful enthusiasts who think theY, are defending the Church by purveying perverten history. Canon Famsanms sees clearly enongn‘ the causes which have given force to the deniann for disestablishinetit. For the last sixty Years the clergy and the Church have been against (knn-icratic progress, the Anglican systoni. has become more exclusive, and the Church 01 England has assumed more and more the features of a sect and less and less those of a national boclY• The Church defence will, he foretells, not diminish but increase the alienation which leads to dis" establishment.

• The Conservative ieaders, Luro Ou.toOury and 3fr Balfour, did not take up a dignified position When waited upon by a deputation of thii Miners' Federation with regard to the Miners' Hours Bill. These two eminent men performed Os feat which is familar to politicians of no very good fixed opinion—that is to say, they " sat upon the fence " more or less ungracefully. But, after al" everybody knows on which site of toe fence Lord Salisbury and Mr. Balfour will come chwu when comes to a question of voting on the Eight llour,sci Bill. Toryism now represdots more than ever it di the cause of vested interests and plutocracy geilerii:- ly, and these are dead against the shortening of toe hours of labour. But doubtless the Tory leaders thought a little diplomacy might be usefully Or. ployed at the present time. The Miners' Federatioa represents a large number of votes, some of might, perhaps, be "caught by chaff" But there is little fear of the miners and their sympathisers be-

ing deluded by these studied ambiguities.

It is a stock argument against the closing Cl 4 public-houses, that such a proceeding cause a decrease of drunkenness in tne district. Although the proposition is absurd on the face of it it is frequently used by the supporters of the liquor., interest. The Lord Chief Justice, Lord Russell 01', Killowpn, is of a totally different opinion, howeve_At ' ' and at the Liverpool Assizes last week.he expre694 his pleasure that there was a decrease in drunkeV ness, which corresp,nded with a decrease in the' number of public-houses. This is evidence fro'l high authority, and as it comes from one of the highest representatives of the judicial bench, should receive the respect of the beer party, vitro belong to the party of "law and order" in th country. ,nda9

If we may judge from the crowded meeting in the 'CO-nperative Hall, last week, there must be a Con- siderable amount of interest felt in Warring- ton in the crusade against anti-compulsory vaccina- tion. But perhaps some of the attendance was due to the fact that sensitive people were warned not to come, a warning which the was admirably calculated to whet ~curiosity of an inquisitive (public. The pictures =shown on the screen wars certainly of a character :which no one—not even the most seasoned—could 'look at with equanimity. It is a long time since '6° many odious, filthy pictures were exhibited in Warrington. The anti-vaccinists alleged that all the unpleasant looking pictures were those of children Who had been vaccinated, and that the ulcers and '3°res were the result of vaccination. It would, how- 'ever, be too rash to jump at conclusions, and before 't)rming a final opinion,it would be as well to heat What Dr. J. Guest Gornall has to say about the „.„ Zatter, at the lecture he promises to give shortly. Anere are certainly two sides to every question,and ualY one was heard on Monday evening.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

DRUNKENNESS.

Before Mr. F. J. Norman J.P., at Runcorn Police Court on Friday morning, Kerhane, Runcorn,was finad ss. including costs for being drunk in Waterloo- road at midnight. LONG SERVICE MEDALS FOR VOLUNTEERS.

At Chester C;;We—d-ne-s-d-a-Y,The following volunteers be- longing to the Runcorn detachment of the 2nd Earl of Chester's Volunteers, were awarded long service medals: —Quarter-master Sergeant W. Hankinson, Colour-ser- geant James Most, Colour-sergeant J. Poole, and Bugler John Buckley. THE GRAVING DOCK QUESTION. .

Mr. F. A. Lake, law clerk to the Urban Council, has received an acknowledgement of his letter to the Man- chester Ship Canal Company, containing the terms of the Runcorn Council, with regard to the graving dock question, which were passed at the meeting on Monday night. The terms will be submitted to the directors at an early date. U.M. FREE CHURCH, RUNCORN. .

A largely-attended meeting of this class was held on Friday evening. rhe chair was occupied by the Rev. J. Stafford. Mr.l3entley went through a very entertain- ing programme, consisting of readings, recitations, &c., which were greatly appreciated, On the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. Edward Jones, he was ac- corded a vote of thanks. BILLIARD MATCH.—RIINCORN V. CREWE. _ - .

On Wednesday the members of the Runcorn Liberal Club journeyed to Crewe to play a billiard match with the members of the Liberal Club at that town. Prior to the match the visitors were regaled with a good tea. The game was 150 up,and 12 aside. At the close it was found that Runcorn had lost by 105 points. The follow- ing are the totals : Runcorn, 1570, Crowe, 1675.
