U.M. FREE CROECH, RUNCORN.

The quarterly Meeting of the Christian Endeavour Society in connection with this church was held on Tuesday evening. The meeting was preceded by a tea at which ab3ot 100 sat down. The Rev. J. Stafford pre- sided at the after proceedings and delivered a very in- teresting address. Mr. Fowlds also addressed the meet- ing. Duets were tendered by Mrs. John Stubbs and Miss Guy ; Miss Emma Stubbs and Miss Newall. Miss Edith Wright recited. BRUNSWICK MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT SOCIETY.

Mr. T. Simister presided at the meeting of this society on Wednesday evening. There was a fair attendance. Toe subject for the evening's programme was' Answer- ing Questions." Mr. Lawton was held responsible. The questions asked were" Vaccination " ; " Does Science and Education lead to the proper care of the masses ?" "Ought bicycles to be taxed ?" and " What influence have the fine arts upon a nation." A discussion followed in which Messrs. T. Leach, William Massey, Leadstone, Jeffrey, Worrall, and G. Hayes took part. Sr. PAUL'S BIBLE CLASS.

In connection with this class on Sunday afternoon an open meeting was held.- The chair was occupied by Mr. B. Kniveton, who gave a Bible reading. The Rev. J. Pulford Williams delivered an interesting address on "The head, heart, and hand." An organ and piano- forte selection was played by Messrs. William Cooke and G. Rowley,Mr. Edward Kniveton sang " 0 had I Jubal's lyre," Mr. Charles Atherton rendered " Jerusalem " with organ Gbligato, Miss Hayes sang" Welcome wanderer," and Miss Edwards gave "Rest." _ _

BAND OF DOPE ENTERTAINMENT AT RUNCORN.

.. In connection with the Primitive Methodist Band of Hope, Runcorn, an entertainment was given in the Greenway-road schoolroom on Wednesday evening. There was a good attendance. Mr. Reynolds occupied the chair and gave an excellent address dealing with temperance. A programme of songs, recitations and a dialogue was gone through. Songs were given by Miss Hillage and Miss Wright. Mr. W. Gittens gave a read- log, and recitations were contributed by :—A. Sayes, A. Stubbs, B. Stubbs, and L. flitchen. A temperance dialogue was taken part in by G. Aston, S. A. Aston, A. Hill, T. Spender and H. Smith. An address was also delivered by Mr. Sayle. VOLUNTEER SHOOTING CLUB, RUNCORN.

In the Drill Hall, Runcorn, the annual meeting of the members of this club was held on Friday evening, for the election of officers. In the absence of C.ptain George Ashton, through indisposition, the chair was occupied by Drill-Instructor Callighan. The president chosen was Major C. W. Hazlehurst ; vice-presidents, the officers of the Runcorn detachment, Captain Harrison of the I Company ,Quartermaster Han ki uson,Messrs. A. Ti m mins, W. B. Frith, G. Shone,with power to add to their num- ber ; treasurer, Sergeant J. A. Rushton ; secretary, Sergeant Woodland. Committee, Colour-sergeants Mort and Poole, Sergeants Edwards and Neild, Corporals Foulkes and Thompson,Privates Houghton and Garratt ; captain of the team, Sergeant Harvey. Matches for the forthcoming season were then arranged. SEAMEN'S INSTITUTE, RUNCORN. There was a very large attendance at the weekly entertainment given in the Seamen's Institute, on Tues- day evening. Mr. W. Shaw, missionary, presided. The programme for the evening consisted of a number of recitations and a reading which were illustrated by means of a number of views shown by the aid of a lime- light lantern. Sr. PATRICK. The anniversary of St. Patrick was observed in St. Edward's Catholic Church on Sunday, when special services were held. Dr. GastAdi delivered an interesting sermon on "St. Patrick," in which he dealt with the life of the noble saint in a v2ry eloquent manner. In the evening he also discoursed on the same subject to a large congreg- ation. Clynch, president, occupied the chair, and delivered a lecture on the life of Saint Patrick, and also referred to the: great evils of drink. On behalf of the society he had pleasure in presenting him with a silver cross. Mr. Quinn spoke of the large heartedness of Mr. Clynch, and the sorrow they felt in parting with him. MISSIONARY. At St. Helens Police Court, on Saturday, before Alder- man Harrison and Councillor J. B. Leach,a middle-aged man named James Charley, of Cowley Hill, was charged with being a dangerous lunatic, It appears that bet- ween five and six o'clock on Friday night Mr. Mundin, the police court missionary, who for several years con- ducted the Shin Canal Mission at Runcorn, was passing the house 'of a man when be heard a setae going on.He inquired what was the matter, and being informed that Mr. Chorley was very violent he entered with a view to talking to him, and getting him to calm down. Mr. Chorley had smashed 's a window with his hasd, from which blood was flowing. Mr. Mundin tried to persuade him to have it bandaged, but he refused and continued his violence. Eventually for the safety of the family and after a struggle he was put into the street, where the police took charge of him. The evidence of Dr. Challoner on Saturday was to the effect that he had been called several times to the man, who had erronious ideas and was riotous. He had been getting slightly worse for eighteen months, and had been drinking excessively for the last fortnight. He was not responsible for his actions, and he (the doctor) would recommend that he be re- manded to the workhouse for a fortnight. —Prisoner said he was very sorry, it was through drink, and be would promise faithfully not to touch it any more.—The Clerk : He speaks rationally now.—Dr. Challener ;My opinion is that he is a dangerous lunatic, and not fit to he at large.—Prisoner was sent to the workhouse to be looked after for a fortnight. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY. MARCH 23. 1895.

RUNCORN MUSICAL SOCIETY.

As will be seen from an advertisement lit another column, the Runcorn Musical Society are to give their last concert of the season in the Public Hall, on Monday evening. The concert will consist of the performance of the Mendelssohn's " Hear my Prayer" and Spohr's "Last Judgment." This latter composition is the earliest of Spolir's three oratorios and was composed about the year 1825. It was first produced before an English audience at the Norwich Musical Festival in 1830 and was received with the greatest possible favour Since that time it has been performed repeatedly and has become very popular. The members of the Runcorn Musical Society have bestowed much time in rehearsing the piece, and there is no doubt that their rendition of it on Monday night will be a credit, alike to the conductor and themselves. The principal artistes will be :—Miss Hetty Creasy, Miss Amy Banks. Mr. E. Dunkerton (Lay Vicar Lincoln Cathedral), and Mr. Ben Grove (principal bass:Foundling Hospital, London). There will be a full orchestra and chorus of 120 voices, under the con- due orship of Mr. F. Crossley. RUNCORN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

A meeting was held in Lacey-et. schoolroom, Widnes, on Tuesday evening, for the purpose of appointing a committee to revise the rules of the Society. Mr. Sayles occupied the chair and there was a large attendance. At the outset, the secretary made a suitable reference to the death of Mr. J. T. W. Mitchell , president of the Wholesale Co-operative Society, and referred to the great interest lis had manifested in the cause of co-oper- ation,and instanced how,that he might further the prin- ciples of co-operation, he had declined to accept the honourable position of director of the Manchester Ship Canal Company. Onthe motion of Mr. W. E Dudley, it was decided to forward a vote of condolence. After a short discussion it was agreed to appoint the following a committee to revise the rules of the Society :—From Widnes, Messrs. Cosier, Powlson, Hughes and Lloyd ; Runcorn, Messrs. Dudley, Moores, Stubbs and Riley ; with four members of the Co-operative Society's Coin- I mittee, • Messrs. Holt, Betney, Aston, Rogerson, and Upton (secretary). It was also decided that they should be paid 24. each per night,•and any expenses incurred. A harmonious meeting was brought to a close by the passing of a Tote of thanks :to the chairman, on the motion of Mr. Cosier, seconded by Mr. Aston. • Sr. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH, RUNCORN.

The Rev. Thcmas Evans (London') delivered his popular lecture on the subject of " John Jones and John Bull" in the above place of worship on Tuesday evening. There was a good attendance notwithstand- ing the inclemency of the weather. The lecturer said that John Bull was certainly the " predominant partner," but he was profoundly ignorant of John Jones. He gave many instances of this, particularly re- ferring to Lord Tennyson's poem where it is stated that the Welsh had not God's Word in their own language. Wales, he replied, had the Sacred Book in their own vernacular long before the poet was born, and its prin- ciples had become the very breath of the Welsh nation. Mr. Evans also gave an able refutation of the charge that Wales was a "nation of liars." It should be better known as the land of the white gloves. The judges had a holiday when they were sufficiently fortunate to be sent to Wales ; they being able to pass from county to county without finding one prisoner alarmed by the assize bugle. In one county there was no prison. He pointed out that the two faults which Wales had most to guard against were those of sectarianism and clannish- ness. The lecturer then discoursed with considerable eloquence on the virtues and failings of John Bull. He I described him as a noble fellow whose hand was ever in his pocket to assist distress. Yet he had need to learn wisdom sven,in his generosity. He might assist the working-man to protect himself against poverty by increasing his weekly wages. The Englishman, he said, was not clannish ; in many large towns, both at home and abroad, colonies of Scotch, Welsh, Irish, Germans, Sm., might be found, but John Bull could be a man amongst men. There were, however, several defects to which it would well repay him to give attention. These were a chronic state of grumbling, the enormous sum of money spent in the liquor traffic, the absurdity of caste, and the spirit of gambling which was fast becoming the curse of every class in English society. The Rev. John Simpson occupied the chair, and proposed a vote of thanks to the lecturer, which was seconded by Mr. W. Carroll. On the motion ot Mr. Evans, supported by Mr. Hewitt, a vote of thanks to Mr. Simpson brought a very. pleasant evening to a close._

===== 0002601_18950323_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCO RN. Bs Otis RUNCORN CORIIESPONDENT.

The time at which the Runcorn Urban Council finished its proceedings on Monday night was per- haps phenomenal in the history of Runcorn. It was almost midnight when the majority of the councillors were able to wend their way home, I say majority advisedly, for it must be stated that a number of councillors—mostly "old hands "—de- parted soon after the Ship Canal business had been carried through, about 10.30 p.m. For three hours, from seven until t-,n o'clock, the Parliamentary ani General Purposes Committee which comprises the whole Couucil,had under consideration the terms of the Manchester Ship Canal Company with regard to what has become known as the Graving Dock question. As intimated in the last issue of the 'a Examiner "their proposals were, with the excep- tion of the provision by the Company of the grav- ing dock, in agreement with those of the Council. The provision of a graving dock • was however, a most important point and the committee were loath to give way in the matter. But, after all, they adopted the wisest course in freeing the Canal Com- pany of their obligation, for if the Company carry out what they have promised, the Council will not have come out of the arrangement empty-handed.

In addition to providing 600 feet of new berth- age, with 24 feet depth of water, the Canal Company are to put into working order the ship repairing slip at the Old Quay, with another slip- way at this point, and also to provide tips for coat and salt and several other conveniences to the advantage of the port. The accommodation for discharging and loading large vessels on the Ship Canal at Runcorn should prove advantageous to the town. There is every prospect that if the large ships navigating the Canal can be provided with good accommodation for unloading at Runcorn, they will stop here, instead of proceeding to Alan- chesth, which in many instances necessitates the cutting of the ship's masts in order to get under the bridges. The goods discharged could be sent to their destination, either by rail, or along the Bridge- water and Ship Canals in smaller craft. Runcorn would thus become a good distributing centre. Employment would be found for many of the •! out- of-works" and new industries might be introduced into the town.

When the Committee's terms, which will be found in another column, were submitted to open council on Monday night, Mr. Walter Collier moved an amendment that the agreement for providing a graving dock by the Company be not waived. The proposition did not, however, meet with much sup- port., for on a vote being taken, only four voted for it, and thus the Committee's.terms were carried by a large majority. The question of the provision of this large graving dock which was in substitution of others destroyed in the'construction of the canal, has been very prominent for some time, and the carpenters have kept the matter to the front. It is of course for the purpose of docking large vessels during repairs, and during construction. But the position of the graving dock, as stipulated in the agreement, would have been entirely on the wrong side of Runcorn bridge. Furthsr, in the construc- tion of large vessels, iron is now fast superseding wood, and it is questionable whether the graving dock would have been as useful as stated. At any rate the Council have asked the Company to reserve land for the purpose, and if there is any hope of a graving dock becoming a useful and profitaMe con- cern, it will without doubt be constructed.

--- It was 10.30 p.m. on Monday evening when the Urban Councillors of Runcorn commenced to take into consideration Mr. Wilson's motion for the powers of the Burial Board to be transferred to the District Council. ,The lateness of the evening did not deter the members—more especially the newly- elected members—from discussing the motion. They stuck to their guns until 11.30, when they agreed to the transference by ten votes to two. It may be thought by some to be ecmomical and good policy to bring the business of the Burial Board under the management of the Council, and thus .make a step towards the centralisation of these little bodies, but it is questionable whether a sug- gestion which was brought forward in support of the motion that a plot of land adjoining the cemetery c.uld be used as a recreation ground, is feasible. What would be thought of one of the most solemn of services being interrupted by the noise of youths, who were playing, say, at football P Fur- thermore, playing balls and other articles would surely be driven into the cemetery with the usual scramble after them. It is also stated that in a short time the land will be required for burial pur- poses, and any expense in converting the field Into a playground would then be a loss to the rate- payers.

You ought always to know what you put into your stomach inthe way of medicine, unless it is precribed by a physician Don't take secret m xtures. They are either dangerous or worthless. Take a remedy like Scott's Emulsion, whose Ingredients are all known to the medical professirn, anp whose formula is endorsed by the whole medical w0r1d,(9.)

THE ACCOUNTS.

Messrs. J. K. Digges and J. Poole were re-appointed to audit the accounts of the Burial Board. It was also decided on the motion of Dr. McDougall, seconded by Mr. Cooke, that the accounts be produced to the auditors on the 4th April, and the adjourned vestry meeting be held on the 18th April.

===== 0002601_18950323_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
:T RE APPOINTMENT OF OVERSEERS.

The Chairman said that might be the last time they would have the opportunity ot appointing overseers at a meeting like tbe present. Under the Local Government Act that duty was vested in every Parish Council, and the parish meeting in the smaller villages ; but the Urban District Councils were left out of the Act, and unless the District Council took the initiative in asking the Local Government Board to give them power to ap- point the overseers, they were unable to do so. The RuncorirDistrict Council was, he understood, to Con- sider the question at the next meeting, and very likely the Council would want to nut themselves on "all fours " with the Parish Councils. It WAS, therefore, very probable that they would not have to make any ap• plication to the justices in future years. Personally he thought they ought to let the existing overseers be the last overseers for the town, seeing they had done their work with such satisfaction to the ratc- payers. (Hear, hear.)—Dr. McDougall proposed the fol- lowing gentlemen from which the magistrates should make the appointments :—Messrs. E. Imison, S. Taylor, James Pritchard, William IMellor, Richard Morris, John Savage, Walter Collier,and George Hewitt. He said they were all business men and well acquainted with commer- cial matters. If that was the last list they would have the opportunity of submitting, let them hope that the next election, even if on a more popular basis, would be made with the same judgment as had been the case dur- ing the last 26 years.—Mr. Digges seconded the proposi tion which was unanimously carried.

RUNCORN RURAL COUNCIL.

At Dutton workhouse on Tuesday, the fortnightly meeting of this Council was held. Mr. Knowles occu- pied the chair and there were also present :—Messrs. J. Weir, C. W. Davenport, T. Wright, T. Lewis, C. Roylance, J. Darbyshire, J. Parr, H. E. Heywood, S. Woodward, J. Spruce, W. Pickering, G. Pilling, W. Bradford, J. Brandreth, W. Holt, E. Wallington, E. Bratherton, J. B. Chadwick, J. M. THE PROPOSED _EXTENSION OF WARRINGTON BOROUGH.

The Local Government Board intimated to the Council that a formal enquiry into the'proposed exten- sion of the borough of Warrington would be held4at the Town Hall, Warrington, on Tuesday, April and in the forenoon.—The Clerk stated that he saw Mr. Potts, the previous day at Chester to inquire what they were going to do in the matter. Mr. Potts told him that the Cheshire County Council were going to oppose the extension,and would be represented by counsel, and Mr. Potts suggested that one would be sufficient for the County Council and the Rural Council. —A petitition from Appleton with 400 signatures against the borough extension was handed in and read. —The Clerk asked for a small committee to be appointed in order that he might consult them as to what steps should be taken to oppose the extension. It was decided that Mr. Davenport, Mr. Knowles, Mr. Brandreth, and Mr. Bradford should form the committee. THE COUNCIL'S INDEBTEDNESS.

A communication was read from the Public Works Loan Board calling attention to an amount of £179 6s. 11d, due to the Board on the 6th inst., and re- questing that the a nount be paid. If the amount was riot paid during the present month their Board would be specified in the annual report to Parliament as in arrears on the 31st inst.—Mr. Brandreth said the amount and others could not be paid because the Council had not got the money. This was owing to the Township of Frodsham not having paid a sum of £4OO which was due on January 4th.--Mr. Williams stated that Frodsham WAS in the same position as Runcorn. There depended upon the chemical works and kindred industries in the neighbourhood of Weston and Runcorn, 50 or 80 persons for their employment, and there had also been a large number of men out of employment for a great number of weeks. It was impossible to get the money in. The bellman was sent round last Saturday threatening that , those who did not pay their rates would be "run in," but this had not had the desired effect. Mr. Jones, the assistant overseer, had told him that he had got a certain amount in, and this would be placed to the credit of the Council as soon as possible.—ln answer to Mr. Darby- shire, the clerk stated that the auditor would not sanc- tion an overdraft at the bank.—The Chairman said the rates should be paid in.—Mr. Braudreth said Frodshain was always behind with its payments.—Mr. Heywood thought it would be wise to write to the Parish Council on the subject, as that authority would have the appointment of the overseers and assistant overseers.— Mr. Darbyshire said the Council must either have the m)ney or have an overdraft.—Mr. Brandreth said the overseers ought to have a banking account, and they should have an overdraft and not the Council.—lt was ultimately decided that the Clerk should write the over- seers on the matter.

THE MANCHESTER SHIP CANAL AND RUNCORN.

THE URBAN COUNCIL'S DECISION.

A special meeting of the Runcorn Urban District Council was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, on Monday evening, for the purpose of consider- ing the advisability of amending the agreement of let September, 1890, re the Graving Dock, &c., and the consideration of the Canal Company's proposals ; and further to determine upon a motion proposed lay Mr. Wilson for the transference of the powers of the Run- corn Burial Board to the District Council from the 25th March. The meeting was of a protracted character, it being after eleven p.m. when the Council rose. Mr. E. Imison, J.P., presided, and all the members of the Coun- cil were present,viz. :—Rev. Dr. Gastaldi,Dr. McKenzie. Messrs. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., S. Taylor, J.P., C.C." J. Pritchard, F. Wiswall, J. W. Lightburn, J.. Wilson, G.' H. Kirkham, R. H. Posnett, W. Collier, H. J. Grine, G. Hewitt, M. Sweetman, T. Dean, D. Bisbrown, J. W. Williams, with Mr. Lake (law clerk), Mr. E. Marshall (secretary), and Mr. Barker (surveyor). ! The following is a copy of the letter received from the Canal Company The Manchester Ship Canal Company, Secretary's Office, 41, Sprihg Gardens, Manchester, March Bth, 1895. Dear Sir,—l now beg to hand you as promised the terms , which were agreed to at the meeting with the members : of your Council at Bridgewater House yesterday, in order that you may submit them to the special meeting , of the Council which it was understood would be sum- moned to meet on Monday next. (1) The Runcorn District Council to waive and fore- : go completely all claims to the provision by the Canal Company of the Graving Dock named in clause 2 sub- section a, of the agreement of the let September, 1890. , (2) Until the wharf referred to in clause 2, subsec- _ .

tion e, of the said agreement is !provided by the Canal Company, the Council to have the privileges granted by clause 3 of the agreement. (3) The Canal Company to amend and put into working order the ship repairing slip, late in the occupa- tion of Messrs. Brundrit and Hayes, at the Old Quay, Runcorn. The said slipway to be made with six ways, and' so that it can be used for three vessels of 130 feet in

length. Steam power to be provided for working the carriages on the said ways. All other proper and neces.- sary machinery, fittings, and appliances, for drawing vessels up the said slipway to be provided in connection therewith, including the use of sheds for materials and tools, appliances for steaming or storing planks, pitch boiler, etc., on reasonable terms. _ _ .

(4) Another slip way to be provided with steam power, either at Castle Rock or Old Quay. (5) The small graving dock near tlsesouth end of the bonded warehouse to be maintained in its present con- dition, and for its present purposes as a graving dock. (6) The public to be allowed to regulate for the use of the said slip ways and graving dock named in para- graphs 3,4, and 5 thereof, in turn with the Canal Co. or others, in the order they may enter in a register to he provided for the purpose. The rates to be charged to the public for such use shall be as follows :—Vessels of 150 tons register and upwards 20s. first day,and 10s. per day thereafter. Vessels of 100 tons register and under 150 tons, 15s. first day and 7s. 6€l. per day thereafter. Vessels of 50 tons and unregistered vessels, and under 100 tons,lo3. for the first day, and ss.per day thereafter. These rates to include the use of steam power,and tackle for drawing vessels up to the said slip ways and back to the water, and for filling and emptying the said graving dock, but all labour, except the services of an engine driver, is to be paid for by the owners of the vessels. The Canal Co. are to undertake no recponsibility in con- nection with the docking, or undocking or otherwise handling the slips. _ _ . . .

(7) The Canal Company to erect at their own ex- pense over the Bridgewater Canal at Halton-road Wharf a footbridge. The fabric of the bridge to be provided by and at the expense of the District Council or other persons, but the abutments and all other works to be provided by the Canal Company. (8) The Canal Company to provide 600 feet of new herthage with 24 feet depth of water, the said berthage to be inclusive of 50 feet alongside the existing Bridge- water jetty, which will be provided by deepening the portion in front of that jetty which has hitherto not been dredged to the full depth. The remainder of the new berthage (i e. in addition to the 50 feet above men- tioned) to be provided by erecting four jetties with 20 feet face to each, at intervals to the westward of the ex- isting Bridgewater jetty. A coal tip is also to be pro- vided by the Canal Company at the westerly end of the new berthage. (9) The Canal Compan-7 to have the option of sub- stituting other equally good accommodation within the Runcorn district for any of the graving slips, subject to reasonable approval by the Runcorn District Council of the new site or sites, and in default of agreement as to such new site or sites, the matter to be settled by arbi- tration. New slips to be provided before old ones done away with. (10) The proposed works to be proceeded with forth- with after the completion of the agreement, and com- pleted within nine months from the date thereof. am happy to inform you that at a meeting of the Board held this afternoon, these terms were reported by the Chairman and confirmed, and lam instructed to say that it is the intention of the directors to take into con- sideration the provision of such further accommodation as may be found necessary to deal satisfactorily with the .business of the port. . _

- The Council may 615- upon the Company pushinz on the works now agreed to with all possible speed. Yours faithfully, A. 11. Whitworth, secretary—F. A. Lake, Eq. Mr. P. Speakman proposed that the term; contained in Mr. Whitwnrth's letter of the Bth March, 1895, for the alterations of the agreement between the Manchester Ship Canal Co. and the Roncorn Improvement Commis- sioners dated the first day of September, 1890, be accepted subject to the following explanations or altera- tions thereof:

That the Council agree to waive the formation of the graving dock mentioned in the agreement of 1890 on condition that the Comgany agree to sell or let a portion of land at some point west of the Railway Bridge within the Runcorn district, adjoining the canal, suitable for the formation of a graving dock equivalent in size to that mentioned in the said agreement, such vie or lease to he to the District Council or their assignees or nominees. The right to take such lease or purchase to be exercised within the term of ten years from the date of the proposed agreement. The site and price or rent to be settled in default of agreement between the parties by arbitration. Item 2. The making of the wharf and works referred to in clause 2 (e) of the said agreement is not to he in definitely postponed, but to be made on or within six months notice from the District Council given at any time hereafter. The rights of user and privileges granted hy clause 3of the said agreement to include the time from the opening of the Canal to the execution of the pro- posed new agreement. The sub-section sof section 79 of the Company's Act of 1885, giving free use of canal for nightsoil, Sz.;., to in- clude freedom from lockage tolls, as the vessels used for such purposes would be within the dimensions mentioned in the 2nd sub-section of the said section. _

',ems, 3 and 4. The slip ways to he provided with cradles or carriages for the carriage of vessels from and to the water.

Item 7. The Company to maintain this bridge. Item 8. • Plans of the proposed new berthage jetties and coal tip to be submitted for approval of the District Council. The coal tip to he capable of loading nat less than 1.000 tons a day. MI costs to he paid for by the Company. lie also proposed that the following gentle- men form a Committee to wait upon the Canal Com- pany: Messrs. Speakman, Lightburn, Imison, Taylor and Pritchard.

n moving the adoption of the report Mr. Speakman said he had great pleasure in doing so. The subject had en- gaged careful attention of the Council for a considerable time. They felt the importance of this settlement and it was decided by almost the unanimous voice of the Coun- cil. It was not from any want of feeling that they had a bargain—a tight bargain—with the Manchester Ship Canal Company. Circumstances had arisen which caused them to feel that it was better to be wise in all their un- dertakings than otherwise. (near, hear.) They had at the present moment a very serious state of deprmsien, which be hoped would soon pass away. As far as he I could help forward—and he was working in that direc- tion—he should lose no time in bringing about something that would assist them materially. The view of the committee was that the Ship Canal had made an agree- ment with them, and a binding one, for the graving dock, and whilst they felt they had a binding agreement with the company, they also felt that there were other interests that they thought would tend to a greater am- ount of labour, if they were carried out. Such was the conclusion they had come to in the report they had brought before them that night. He felt sure that the future of Runcorn, if it was anything like what was premised as to large ships coming up, the question of a graving dock would force itself upon the Ship Canal Company. Such bad been the case in Liverpool on the Mersey Harbour Board. It was important that they should have within a very short space of time the slip wave put in which were named in the report, and that they should have an extended lay-by, whereby vessels would not have to stay at Saltport, but come into their district, whereby they would have the advantage of them, and if se,they would have additional employment for their labourers, who wanted work. Mr. Pritchaed seconded the motion. One immediate benefit would, slid, be that work would be commenced almost immediately, arid they had a hope that it• would tend to the lessening of the depression. The work had to be completed within nine months.

Mr. Collier moved as an amendment that they adhere to the conditions contained in Mr. Lake's previo.v, let ter to Mr. Collier, M Manchester. He was not speaking his individual opinion, because he knew the committre had met from time to time and had given the subject mature consideratioi., hut now they had upset all their calcula- tions, They had no right to. waive the provision of the graving dock altogether. He thought the Ship Canal

Company had not o/ered any reasonable excuse why their agreement should be cancelled. Mr. Dean seconded. Mr. Lightburn thought if: Mr. Collier had been there at the commencement of the proceedings, and had beard the arguments used, he would have had sufficient intel- ligencejnot:to move that amendment. Mr. Once thought, taking all the circumstances into consideration, and without going into the particulars, with whicn they were all well acquainted, as to the state of trade, &c., that the committee had done the best they could for the town, and taken the wisest course in adopt- ing the recommendations placed before them.

The Rev. Dr,Gastaldi said he had become converted to

what he considered the right pa9b. He was desirous that their decision should be for the greatest good to the greatest number, and under present circumstances they should accept the proposals contained in the recom- mendations decided upon by the committee. Mr. Bisbrown thought he could claim the question as his in a peculiar sense. Through thick and thin for a number, of years he had brought the matter before the Board and pressed it forward for solution, and he was free to admit that he never approached a subject with a . keener sense of responsibility than that, and yet he had not the slightest doubt that what they proposed doing , was the right course to take, considering the ix collar circumstances and the financial position of thjs Ship Canal Company. He thought they were acting on the principle of the greatest good to the greatest number. They were acquainted with the well known fable of the dog which snatched at the shadow and missed the sub- stance. He thought their wisest course was to accept the proposals, for he believed it would not only give employment to a large number of shipwrights,but boiler makers and painters would be engaged. Mr. Bythell said at the Bridgewater House that they would corn- mence the work at once, and also remarked that they had a large fleet of dredgers, pontoons and steamers, which would be repaired in the town. That wodld give employment for a great number of men. On the amendment being put Messrs. Collier, Dean, Sweetman and Kirkham voted for it, but the other 14 members present voted in favour of the original resolu- tion. It was thus carried 11V 3 votes to four. THE QUESTION OF T AKIN], OVER IDE BURIAL BOARD.

Mr. Wilson moved "That under Section 62 of the Local Government Act. 1894, it is resolved that the powers of the Runcorn Burial Board shall feie transferred to the District Council as from the 25th day of March inst." He thought it was a thing they ought to do. Some people who were always looking into the past,said they were taking a leap into the dark, but in this pro- gressive. age they should let the dead past bury its dead; ' they should go forward in the spirit of the times. Mr. Collier seconded.

The Rev. Dr. Gsstaldi thought it was a very geod thing that the proposition should have been brought for- ward, and he supported it. Mr. Speakman regretted exceedingly that the metion had been brought up at an late an hour, when the great body of members had left in consequence of being there go long on the previous subject. Be thought Mr. Wilson was quite within his rights, having given notice of the mfetion, but for the Council to act in that manner,when the case had not been put, nor the figures properly stated, he had no hesitation in saying that during the 20 years he had been connected with the Board he had never known such a leap in the dark as the present one. They had in the secretary of the Burial Board an aged servant of the town, and to disturb him was the greatest possible unkindness. The Council had a great amou as of work on its hands, not only now, but more w u quickly coming upon them. Then again,it would meag an additional burden on the ratepavers. for the raisin of the money for the maintenance of the cemetery would be transferred from the poor rate to the general district rate. Yet that action was called progressive. He did not oppose the question from a parsimonious spirit, but because the case had not been made out. Mr. Posnett thought that the remarks of Mr. Speak- man should not go without some reply with regard to the disturbance of the secretary of the Burial Board, the remarks were not warranted, and especially after Mr. Wilson had elven such a lucid explanation. Therefore, he thought they should not go uncentradicted. The motion made it dear that there would be no disturb- ance of the secretary of the Burial Board,and he did not see why such a statement should he dragged in. Then Mr. Speakman had referred to certain members being away. If they did not think it of sufficient importance, those who had gone were themselves responsible, and it was a matter tor the ratepayers, and not f r those mem- bers nre.ent to discuss. With regard to the point about an additional burden on the ratepayers,he (Mr. Posnett) did not think such would be the case. He thought Mr. Wilson in moving the resolution in committee made it I:Trier:Hy clear that pltimately there would be a saving to the ratepayers of something like £5O a year. The change was putting the Cemetery on a more democratic basis. He added he for one 114 d been very !much inter- ested in the obtaining of a recreation ground for Run- corn. He remembered saying to Mr. Wiswall that he would do ell he could for getting the site of the Ceme- tery foe the children. He thought that by placing the BuriAl Board in the hands of the Council they would I have better opnertunitieS for dbtaining, that greund for the people of Runcerra as a recreation ground. He gave those fur or five reasons in support of Mr. Wilson's m .

The Chairman thought it was a little un- reasonable to be debating the subject at so late an hour as eight minutes past eleven o'clock. He must congratulate the members on their physical endurame. He never heard such a subject introduced with less arguments or less information behind it. He was inclined to think that the recreation ground had more to do with the subject than anything else. He ventured to think that when th ground became t lie Property of that Council they would take the same views as the E-urial Board. The Council appeared to be dis- playing a voracious appetite for work. He thought they would bave plenty to do for the next twelve months but if they were ready to take over those works, no one would dispute their rights, but they would question their wisdom.

Mr. Wilson having replied, the following members v6terl for the proposition :—Messrs. Wiswall. Wilson, Kirkham, Posnett, Collier, Sweetman, Dean, Bisbrown, Williams, and the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi (10) and Mr. Spealiman and the Chairman voted against. This concluded the business about half an hour befor midnight.

A RUNCORN PUBLIC-ROUSE TRANS- ACTION.

In the Nisi Prius Division of the Liverpool Assizes on Monday, before Mr. Justice Lawrance, the case of Guzz- well v. the North Cheshire Brewery Company, Limited, came on for hearing. The plaintiff in this case was Cap- tain Guzzwell. of Grimsby, and the action was brought to recover £lOO and the return of a promisors' note for £l4O from the defendants, who, however, denied that the money was repayable, and counterclaimed £lOO for breach of covenant. Mr. Mulholland, Q.C., and Mr. florridroa appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Bigham, Q C., and Mr. LAZtrus-Langtion for the defendant com- pany. whose offices are in Macclesfield, Plaintiff said that having seen an advertisement res- pecting a splendidly fitted-up public-house to let near Runcorn and close to the Manchester Ship Cenal, he went down to see the place with Mr. Harrison, of Old- ham-st., Manchester. Plaintiff took his wife with him. They were led to believe that the place was doing a good : business, and that it would improve when some .works , were finished close by. In the end he consented to pay £175 for the inventory and goodwill, and £73 for the stock, on the understanding that he could give the house up after a month's trial and have his money returned. He then signed an agreement,which he did not read,and which was not read over to bun. Ile paid £lOO down. and along with his wife signed a promisory note for £175 At the end of a month's time plaintiff was dissatisfied and gave up the houso. He claimed the return of the money. hut the defendant company would only give him £132 for what he had agreed to pay £248. He stated that he signed the agreement on the advice of Mr. Harrison, who said that he was acting for him as aeent, and to whom £l2 was paid as commission.— Cross-examined, plaintiff denied that the agreement was read over to him in the presence of Mr. Harrison and Mr. Edwards, the manager of the defendant com- pany. Mr. Bigham ridiculed tha idea that the defendants would make a sale of the house,turn out their manager, and go to the expense of having an agreement drawn up, on the understanding that plaintiff, if he did not like the bargain, could go out, leave the place empty, , and have his money handed back to him without the crmpany deducting a penny. No such thing was agreed upon, and not a word of it appeared in the agreement which, it was contended, was read over to the plaintiff before he signed it. _

Walter ffarrison nenied that he had promised that the defendant would return plaintiff his money if in a month's time he did not like the house. ‘Vitness read the agreement over to plaintiff, and also told him that he had read in the papers that there was to be a graving dock in that neighbourhood. His Lordship said that the only question for the jury was whether there existed a contract entitling the nlaintiff to have his money back and to give up the house if he did not like his bargain in a month's time. The jury found for the defendant company.

THE DROWNING OF A DREDGER CAPTAIN.—At the Weaver Hotel, Weston, Point, on Friday afternoon, Mr. J. E. Worsley held an inquest on the body of John Woodhouse, Ellesmere Port, who had been missing since December 29th.—Evidence of identification was given by the deceased's wife.—John Sullivan, mate of the • dredger Irk, spoke to finding the body floating in the Ship Canal on the previous morning. He had a rope attached to it, and assisted to convey it to the Weaver Hotel, where he handed it to the police. The body was found near where the dredger Barry was working on the night he was missed, which was very boisterous. A fearful storm raged, and it was snowing.—Henry Musker. Weston Point, master of the dredger Barry,said he had known Captain Woodhouse over two years. On the night of December 29, about eight o'clock, he was at his house, and said lie would return in a few minutes, after visiting the Dock Hotel. He asked Woodhouse to return to his house. Decessed had a particular dread of Weston Point, and thought the water should.be railed off for the protection of sailors. He was not a man who took much drink.—Peter Coyle, landlord of the Weaver Hotel, deposed to the captain coming to his house about eight o'clock on the night in question, and leaving about 9.45. Witness said he could have a bed, but he said he was capable of going aboard, and should not sleep except on his boat. He had four two•pennyworths of whiskey, but was sober so far as aould be seen. He started to go at nine o'clock, but in consequence of the hailstorm he returned. He was capable of taking care of himself.— By a juror: Deceased and a man named Williams,. who had been missing since the same night, were not togs- ther.—P.C. Hatnmersley describedithe condition of the body. There was a frightful gash on one side of the head, and the right thigh and an arm were broken, but these had evidently been caused afterdeath. There was money in his pockets and also his watch, which had stopped at 1.25. He saw tha deceased about ten o'clock on the night of December 29th, going in the direction of Mr. Musker's house, when he was alone. He did not consider there was any connection between the two men being missing.—.1 verdict of "Found drowned" was returned.

BEFORE TM, MAGISTRATES.

GROSS NEGLECT OF CHILDREN. Matthew and Eliziteth Carley, Pierpoint-street, were I charged at the Warrington Borough Court on Friday.last week with neglecting their children in a manner likely:to cause:them suffering,on January 24,and January 29. Mr. Buckton who appeared on behalf of the N.S.Y.C.C., said that the case had been before the magistrates twice. On February Ist, the case was adjourned for a month to give the defendant a chance to reform, and when it was called again it was further adjourned for a fortnight because the defendants did not appear. The defendants had eleven children ranging in age from 21 to five. The children whom they were summoned for were respectively 10, 9,8, 6, and 5 years. At the time they were originally summoned, the defendants lived at Dallam-lane, but had since removed to Pierpoint-street. There had been no improvement whatever in the condition of the children since the case was ad- journed. The Act under which they had been summoned gave the magistrates power to inflict both imprisonment and a fine in addition. If the Bench could only realise the state of the houses of defendants in similar cases to the present one, they would see that they were very serious cases indeed. It was difficult to give the real state of affairs by calmly reciting them. —Dr. Gornall : You don't imply that I am wanting in knowledge of them ?—Mr. Buckton : Oh, no. lam speaking generally.—Alderman Wright : I think the Inspector lays it down pretty strongly. (Laughter.)—Mr. Buckton : But not too strongly. From my own know- ledge he rather understates it.—lnspector McAteer then described the state of the house in similar terms to those previously reported. He said that the children were in the same filthy condition now as they were then, and were not provided with proper food or clothes The an earned 30s. per week, and gave his wife a sovereign. He did not press the case against the male defendant.—Cnrroborative evidence was given by P.C.'s Williamson and Meacock.—The husband said that the condition of his house was not so bad as described by the inspector. He only got 30s. per week when in full work, which was very rarely. In fact last week, he had not worked at alt.—the Bench decided to dismiss the case against the Male defendant, and to fine the female defendant 103. and costs, or in default 14 days. RUNCSFIN FLLTMAN ROBBED:7-TRE CULPRIT SEVERELY

DEALT Wiry.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Saturday morning before Messrs. Burgess and Geddes, Winifred Burke, 8 Hill-street, was clearged with being drunk and disorderly in Buttermarket.street, on Friday afternoon ; she was further charged with stealing from the person of Thomas Hindley, on Thursday, the 14th inst., the sum of £4. The prisoner, who is a well-known charac- ter, appeared in the box with one eye draped in deep mourning and a cheek was plaistered in a very compre- hensive manner. She pleaded guilty.—Mr. :Hunt the Chief Constable) stated that the complainant, Thomas Hindley, was the captain of the fiat" Caldwell "from Runcorn, and resided at 14 Cowley-street, of that town. Defendant was a native of St. Helens and had been coming between that to vu ad Wareingten for some years. On ti is afternoon or Thiireday the 14th the complainant finished unloading a flat laden with slates for the Warrington Slate Company,and afterwards went to the offices of that Company. and received a cheque for £l3 29. which he cashed in gold. Complain- ant had a friend with him, John Dunhobbin, and about 6 o'clock, they were in the Roebuck Inn and had some drink together and complain-'1 ant treated " defendant and other women who were there. They got " jelly," and about seven o'clock the complainant dropped asleep all.l awok elem.: eight o'clock when the women were gone, out Dunbobbin re- mained. He felt in his vest pocket and found that VS were gone. After informing the landlord the complain- ant reported his loss at the police station. Detective Monaghan made a search and found the party of women at the " Lower Angel" on Friday, also aecer- taining that the defendant changed a sovereign and had left a purs4 containing 30i. in the oars of a barman, named James Winders. When the defendant was charged with the theft she. replied that it was a man from Bolton who had stolen the money and she had re- ceived £4 as her share.—This was her 20.11 appearance in court, and the magistrates sent her to gaol for three months' hard lab.- ur. The Chief Constable and Mr. Bu- rgess then cautioned the barman about the serious folly he was guilty of in receiving the money from the defend • ant. His carelessness might have been to the prejudice of his employer. Winders acknowledged his mistaken kindness and left the court with promises of future care- fulness.

ANOTIIER WORKMAN CAJOLED AND ROBBED. .On Tuesday morning at the Borough Police Court,be- fore Mr. R. Garnett (chairman) and Mr. E J. Guest, Annie Ryan,well known to the police, was charged with stealing from the person of Benjamin Bailey a purse con- taining 10a., on Monday evening.—From the evidence , produced it appeared that on Monday the complainant, who resides at 181, Winwick-road, met the prisoner and another woman in Bridge-st., and at their request he went into the " Star and Garter" puNio-house and treated them. After paying, the complainant went out with Annie Ryan into Shuttle-st., near to where the pri- soner lodged. While going down the passage the prisoner put her arm affectionately round comolainant,and while doing so managed to abstract his purse from his trousers pocket. 'Then,to give him something to do,,he knocked his cap off, and bolted. Complainant reported his loss to Detective Monaghan, and in company with him axd P.C. Evans went to the " Old House at Home," where the complainant pointed out prisoner with other women. She was arrested, and is. 2d. taken out of her hand. When searched the purse was not found upon her.— Benjamin Bailey, the complainant, corroborated this eviderte, and Mrs. Mullins, the landlady of the " Star and Garter" deposed to serving the party with drinks. Detective Monaghan stated that when apprehm ded the prisoner was with three other women, and on tne way to the Police Station was very violent.—The magistr ttes said they had no sympathy with the complainant, but sent the prisoner to gaol for one month's hard labour. She accepted this sentence with great good humour, hn- rnediately bursting into song for the edification of the Bench.

Two PAYMENT 3 Fort OS'S JOB. At the Borough Polke Ceurt, on Tuesday morning, before Mr. R. Garnett and Mr. E. J. Guest, John Edwards, 38 Cockhedge-lane, was charges with stealing by means of a trick 53. 4d. from Mr. Smethurst, jun., oa Wednesday the 13111 inst.—Mr. Hunt state i that tha complainant was James Henry Smethurst, jun., 4, Bewsey-road, and a few days ago complainant's father gave defendant a job to whitewash the outbuildings of a couple of houses. When he had completed the job de- fendant went to Mr. Smathurt's agent,Mr. James Bolton, accountant, Sankey-street, who paid him 84. 4d. on Wednesday the 13th. On the same day the defendant, accompanied with his wife, went to complainant and represented that he had finished the job, and on his ac- count he was paid 54. 4d. by Mr. Smethurst, jun. On the following Monday the complainant and Mr. Bolton were going over the account and they then discovered that each had paid the defendant fer the same job. The matter was then reported to Detective Monaghan who afterwards apprehended the dcfendant.—Mr. Bolton corroborated, and Mr. Smethurst, jun., deposed to pay- ing defendant's wife in his, presence the Sc. 4d. as de- fendant was not sober.—Detective Monaghan having given evidence, the defendant was sent to gaol for we month's hard labour.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARI

UNITED ALKALI COMPANY' LIMITED.

MEETING OF SHARE HOLDERS. STORMY PROCEEDINGS. The fourth annual general meeting of the United Alkali Company, Limited, was held on Friday afternoon last week, in the Law Association R 901119. Cook-street. Liverpool, Sir Charles Tennant, Bart., president, occupy- ing the chair. There was a very large number of share- holders present, and a considerable number were un- able to make their way into the room, which was crowded some minutes previous to the commencement

of th 3 business. The President said it had hitherto been a source of pleasure and satisfaction to him to occupy the chair, but he could not say that he associated either ot those feelings that day in asking their consent and approval to a much more adverse state of affairs than he had ex- pected at the last meeting. No doubt the year '94 had been a very had year commercially and industrially, but no trade perhaps had suffered more than that with which they were identified, viz., the alkali branch of

the chemical trade. Mr. John Brock, chairman of the directors, in the course of his speech, said he was riot prepared for the extreme depression which crept over business as the year grew older, amounting in its closing months to almost complete collapse in some branches. The markets all round last year were extremely unsatis- factory, and they had to be content with delivering a much smaller proportion of their output than usual. Nor was it a question of price; is was simply that in the then condition of trade the world aid not want their goods. New processes and the march Of invention— espechlly electrical processes—had not been lost sight of, and everything in this direction likely to tis useful and profitable had been carefully examined, an iso far they had found everything in this direction so decidedly an improvement as to inatica then to adopt it. Their various works had been kept in thorough repair and efficiency, and they had paid the cost out of revenue. As to the future there were certainly signs in some dir- ections of improving trade; but they needed a good deal ot development before it would be safe to speak in any decidedly hopeful way about them. (Rear. hear.) The chairman concluded by moving the adoption of the report, and declaration of a dividend of 7s. per share on the preference shares, making, with the interim dividend paid in August, the sum of 7 per cent, per annum.

Mr. E. Muspratt seconded, and said they could com- mand the comsumption of the world. If the articles they produced were consumed they could manufacture them as cheaply as any other firm in the world. Refer- ence had been 104de to Messrs. Brunner, Mond and Co., and, of course, their manufactories were not exactly the same as those of the United Alktli Company; and they had undoubtedly suffered, so far as consumption was concerned, to a far greater extent than that firm. (A Shareholder: " They are practical men, and can make the work pay if you cannot.") Mr. Piggott saicl that shares that had stood at £8 twelve months ago were now quoted at £2, and some of the directors had evidently anticipated a fall, and knew when to sell out at the best price. They ought to be ashamed of themselves. (Applause.) Everyone would admit that there were too many large salaries and too many on the beard of those whose works had been taken over by the company. He had been connected with commercial affairs since he was eight years of age, and everything he had touched, with the exception of this company, had turned into money. He was sorry to say that he bad lost £l,lOO in twelve months by this com- pany. Ele would like to know what answer the direc- tors had got for selling their shares ? (Applause.) rho President said he was originally the largest share- holder in the company, and that day he was a larger shareholder than ever he had been. He had never sold one share. Mr. Piggott seemed to have been addressing him personally. Mr. Piggott: Because you made the opening speech. . . . .

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of the above Board was held on Wednesday in the Workhouse, when the chair was occupied by Mr. W. Houghton. There were also present, Mrs. Clay, Revs. G. F. Roberts, J. H. Riddette, and T. R. Matthews, Messrs. S. Davies, G. Pilling, A. J. Jolley, W. Hickman, W. Cross, A. Jolley, E. L. Bent, T. B. Malkin, A. Lang, J. Warburton, T. Mather, T. Halton, J. W. Knowles, J. Clarke, J. Dignura, H. Hibbert ; J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (deputy clerk), C. H. Ward (master), and H. M. Mines (superintend- ent).

THE CALLS.

The ASSISTANT CLEax reported that the time for the payment of the third and last calls of the townships was long overdue. Rixton and Southworth-with-Croft were still in arrears. It was highly essential that the calls should be paid before the 24th March.—lt was directed that the attention of the overseers of the town- ships mentioned should be called to the matter.

SELECTION OF ASSISTANT TO THE woßsHocrste MATRON.

At a meeting of the House Committee, held on March Eith, the following were selected to appear before them Lir the post of assistant to the matron, viz : Miss Catterell {3B), Miss Lockhart (32), and MiS3 Dew (40). It was recommended that the American organ offered for 2.10 10s. be purchased frem Mr. Thomas Sutton. A vote of thanks was accorded Mr. Thompson for pro- viding a concert to the inmates. At a subsequent meet- ing, Miss Lockhart was recommended for the office of assistant to the matron on the terms advertised.—The minutes were passed on the motion of Mr. DAVIES bccended l.)y, Mr. KNOWLES.

A LRTTER FROM pm) MEMBER OF TFIE BOARD.

The CLEIIX said that he had received a letter trom Mr. Hood Wright, an old member of the Board, who had recently been elected upon the Frome Board of Guar- dians. Mr. Wright asked for information with regare to the food supptied te tb,- inmates. He said that he thought that that supplied to the inmates at Frome was inferior to that at Warrington.—lt was directed that the information asked for be supplied.

MINOR CASES.

WARRT,NGTON BOROUGH COURT.

FRlDAY.—Before Alderman Harrison (chairman) Alder- man Wright, Mr. J. H. Fleming, Dr. Garnall, Mr. S. Wright, Councillor H. Roberts. TRAVELLING WITHOUT TICKET ON THE RAILWAY.—Peter : Fernley, 11 Philip-st., was charged with travelling, from Sankey Bridges to Warrington, on February 23, without having previously paid his fare. He was also charged with committing the same offence on Fenruary 25. Mr. Fenna, solicitor, said that the defendant was employed by Morton and Co., electric engineers, and was formerly employed by the Railway Comna,ny in the telegraph department, and therefore well knew how to go about. There was no doubt that the defendant had systematically done thin thing. Unfortunately the chief witness, ticket collector Williams,for the offence on 23r February was unable to attend owing to illness. The defendant. came on that date by the, 12.15 train from Widnes, and went along the line in company with a num- ber of men who werked on the railway. He was seen by William Booth, foreman porter, and he sent ticket collector Williams after him, but he missed him. On February 25th, defendant got out of the 6.10 p.m. • train and again went along the line towards the Slutchers-lane crossing. Ticket co'- lector Holland who saw him went round by Arpley-st. and found on reaching Slutchers-lane crossing that the defendant had gone on towards Arpley Station. Holland followed him and caught him, and defendant then said that he had not come by train. Evidence was given in support of the charge by Ticket-collector Hol- land, William Booth, and George Barratt.—The defend- ant said that he had simply gone on the station to SPH a youth, and denied that he had travelled by traira.—The magistrates considered the case proved, and inflicted a fine of ss. and costs for each offence. ONE as BAD as THE OTHER.—Catherine Davies, Sharp- st., was charged with assaulting and heating Frances Brocklehurst, also of sharp-st., on March 11. Mrs. Brocklehurst in a very excited manner said that Mrs. Davies lived opposite to her, and was continually mak- ing game of her. On the aboYe date, she told Mrs. Davies to mind her own business, whereupon the latter challenged her out to fight. A scuffle ensued, during which Mrs. Davies scratched her (complainant) in the face.—Mrs. Davies said that Mts. Brocklehurst was the offender, for she struck the first blow, and also pulled out a large quantity of her hair which she pro- duced, and which left a bald patch on her head. She brought two witnesses to prove this.—The magistrates considered that both sides were to blame, and that one was as bad as the other. They,therefore,dismissed the case.

RUNCORN PErTY SESSIONS.

MONDAY.—Before Major ilazlehnrst (chalrm3o) Mr. P. Speakinati..l P.. C.C. and Mr. James Elandley. TRANSFER OF LICENSES. —The license of the Ship Inn, Reath-road, was transferred from Philip Davies to Joseph Smith and Elias Hurst was also granted per- mission to sell at the Oddfellows Arms,Wellington-street in lieu rf John Shaw. NON-PAYMENT OF RATES.—Eighteen persons were summoned for non-payment of Poor Rate ; three non- payment of District Rate ; and ten non-payment of water rate. In some instances the money was paid whilst in other orders for imtnediate parment were made. Mr. Speakman did not sit on the Bench during the hearing of these cases.

CHAROII OF Asseum—An elderly person named Eliza- beth Richardson was charged with assaulting Mary Jane Pee on the lath March. In rep'y tithe charge she admitted striking the complainant.—Mrs. Pye stated that she kept a greengrocer's shop and the assault com- plained of occurred in Hughes Court. The defendant owed her some money for goods, and on the date named witness went to ask her for it. Instead of handing her the money she struck her in the face with her fist. Tire defendant had previously insulted her. She could not pay her way through people owing her mon-y and had been summoned for non-payment of water rate.—The defendant said she was sorry for what had occurred,but the complainant had brought it upon herself by calling her, and casting imputations against her character. As it was the first offence against the defendant the magis- trates discharged her, and relieved the complainant of the costs.

A DISGUSTING WOMAN.—Margaret Crank, Brunswick- st., Weaded guilty to soliciting in Leinster Gardens on the 1.8:11 inst.—P.C. Richardson proved the case. It was the prisoner's first offence, and the Chairman stat-,d that the magistrates had decided to give her a chance to turn over a new leaf, and would discharge her.

- A Shareholder remarked that if Mr. eiggotr, he'd stuck to his shares he would not have lost his money. Mr. Piggott : I have stuck to them. I have them here. (Laughter.) Mr. Kavannagh said he had put his :money into the company and had it there still because the directors were men who were respected in :Lancashire. The gentlemen connected with the concern had a great responsibility to prove that it was not a bogus cempany, as stated by the" Financial News." The Ship Canal Company, which paid debenture interest out of capital and had no pros- pect of paying a single halfpenny en the ordinary shares stood as high ia the public estimation as the United Alkali Company. Mr. Thomas Snape, M.P., one of the directors, said he had heard the remarks of the first speaker, who men- tioned that the directors had been selling their shares. (A Voice : 'A fact.") He had discovered that this was one of the contributory causes of the fall in the value of the shares, and when he went on to investigate the matter he found that it was true to this extent, that two of the directors had sold their shares, and on the althority of the books of the company he found that one , of the directors—(cries of " Name")—when the concern was launched, held 1234 preference shares and 1562 or- dinary shares, while at the present day he had 200 ordin- ary shares only. He would tell them why he was still a director—(interruption). According to the articles of association,a director was obliged to hold £lOOO nominal value—(& Voice : "Chuck him ")—and if it were not for the word " nominal" he would be disqualified from sitting on the board. He (Mr. Snape) held that though that director might be protected in his seat by the letter of the law, under the spirit of the articles of association he held stock worth only £425. That gentleman's name appeared in the report as offering himself for re-election. Be was bound to do so according to the articles of association. He did not know whether he intended to press forward his election, but if he did so he (the speaker) would take the sense of the meeting upon it. (Hear, hear.) There was another director whose name did not appear on the report. He was re-elected last year, and had held originally 3,437 preference shares and 5,141 ordinary shares, but now, like the other director, he had only 200 ordinary shares. (Cries of "Name.") Be would men- tion his name later on. Be intended to submit a reso- lution in accordance with clause 93 of the articles of association, which was as follows :—"The company may, by an extraordinary resolution, remove any director before the expiration of his period of office, and appoint another qualified person in his stead." He intended to ask the meeting to accept a resolution removing that gentleman from his position. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Holbrook Gasket!, sen., said nothing would have induced him to rise but the fact that a certain notorious paper had published his name as having transferred—(a Voice: "Sold ")—shares to a considerable extent.. The fact was that he had not sold a share since that com- pany was formed. ' On the contrary, be had bought shares, and if any of them were weak enough or foolish enough to part with their shares at the present absurd prices, they would find some of them transferred and registered to him. He had seven sons and daughters. and each of them was a shareholder in the company. Members of his family had from time to time bought shares, and they meant to do so still. They might take that as a fair exiiinple of the action of the great body ef direc:ors. No doubt Mr. Snape had spotted two of the directors; he had ho feeling in their favour, but left them in the hands of the sharehelders ; but let them not go away with the idea that that conduct had been general on the board. As tar as he was concerned, if he lived, he would be a larger shareholder next year than he was that day. (Applause.) Alter some discussion the report and accounts were unanimously adopted. The next business was the election of directors, the retiring ones being Messrs. E. Baxter, H. Gaskell, jun., D. WlCischnie, Charles Wigg, and P. J. Worsley. In the course of his statement upon the estimates for the half-year ending September,lB9s,the Clerk said that the estimates had no effect upon the outlay. If they proved to be in excess,then the surplus went to ease the burden of a future rate. In the undesirable event of a deficiency,then increases of future rates were inevitable. For in-maintenance the estimate he advised was £2,000, namely, at workhouse. £1,300; at schools, £145. For out-door relief. £l,BOO. For lunatics, £1,600 ; non- resident poor, £l5 ; extra medical fees, £l5 ; vaccina- tion, £175 ; registration,£l6o ; salaries and superannua- tion, £1,475 ; Assessment Committee expenses, £250 ; legal expenses and apprehension of offenders, £5O ; miscellaneous expenses, inclusive of conveyances to the house and hospitals,paupers in other institutions, visita- tion of pauper luuatics,inealment and interest of loans, sohool fees for non-pauper children, removal and settle- ment expenses, subscriptions to infirmaries, furniture and property building and repairs, conference expenses, rent of pay stations, establishment charges, printing, stationery, and advertising, insurance, £1,250 ; the total estimate of common fund expenditure being thus £9.015. This estimate was, however, to be reduced by £2,470, being one-half of the grants received through the Lancashire County Council on account of officers' eateries and for part maintenance of lunatics, leaving £6,545 chargeable on townships according to rateable value. To this sum must be added for county rates £3,872 chargeable upon the townships in the proportions fixed by the county rate precept. The townships of Warrington, Newton, and Latchford were further to be charged with the salaries of collectors of poor rates. There was also provision made by calculation of an additional one-sixth part of the common charges to meet the demands upon the Board in the first -month of the following half-year. On the other hand they were in a position to apply £1,917, as estimated balance at the close of the present half-year, to the townships, hence the actual call upon each township would be considerably less in ptoportion to the rateable value than the calls by their predecessors fer the preseet half-year. Duriog the year, there had been a most unreasonable action at law against the Board for alleged false im- prisonment. For nearly a whole year the Guardians had been dragged on as parties to the suit. Finally upon a suggestion made that they consented to claim no costs from the plaintiff if he discontinued the action, yet with a perfect knowledge that to attempt to recover costs would be to threw away good money after bad, all pro- posals of the kind were rejected. 'the action had been since discontinued against the Guardians, and also against their officers, and a shameful waste of public money had been occasioned by the procedure against them.—Upon the motion of the chairman, the report of the Clerk was adopted and ordered to be printed. It was also decided that Mr. Shearer's expenses incurred in the action of Miller v. the Guardians and others, be defrayed by the Union. VACANCIES ON VARIOUS COMMITTEES.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON

SERVICES FOR THE PEOPLE. The usual People's Service was held in the Bold-sf. Wesleyan Church, on Sunday afternoon, and was con- ducted by Mr. John Volt. The address was given by Mr. John on. of Palgate, who said his subji-ict was "Sympathy." He referred to the deplorable lack of this " feeling for others "at the present time, and spoke of the immense possibilities of well-directed sympathy. He showed by means of a fable, that far more good might be accomplished by sympathy than by unkindness, and made use of several quotations, in order to show that the most successful po,its and writer,' were tho,e who had the greatest power to see and to feel for others. ND book, he said, was so full of sympathy as the Bible, and he urged all to come into personal contact with Jesus Christ the greatest sympathiser who ever lived. Musicil selec- tions were well rendered by the orchestral band, and considerable skill was manifested by the manner in which Mr. Corbett sang" Remember thy Creator."

RUNCORN BOARD OF OUARDIANS.

A meeting of this Board was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. Knowles occupied the chair and in addition to the members of the Rural Council present there were :—Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. T. Handley, J.P., A. Norman, S. Yarwood, T. Lea, and J. Ashton (clerk) G. Ashton (deputy clerk).

THE REGISTRARSHIP OF RUNCORN.

A communication was read from the General Register office, Somerset Elouse,stating that the Registrar General had confirmed the appointment of Mr. Thomas Cooke, to the office of registrar of births and deaths for Runcorn sub-district. With reference to the vacancy in the office of Registrar at Marriages, caused by the resignation of Mr.Coleburn,it was asked whether it was desirable to fill the vacancy,aud it was pointed out that Mr. Cook as re- lieving officer could not be appointed without the con- sent of the Local Government Board.—The Clerk stated that he had written to the Local Government Board on I the matter, but had not yet received a reply.

TEIR _TEA. FOR OLD ROUEN:

A letter was read from Mrs. Marsden stating that there seemed to be some misunderstanding as to the tea for old women. At the last meeting of the Ladies' Visiting Committee each lady present agreed to contri- bute a proportion. It was not thought needful to make an entry to the effect in the minute book.

ILLNESS OF THE CHAIRMAN.

A letter was read stating that Mr. Mclntyre was con- fined to his bed, and would be unable to attend to any business for sometime. The doctor had ordered him to be perfectly free from any business.—Mr. Woodward moved, and Mr. Bradford seconded, and it was carried unanimously, that an expression of sympathy be for- warded to Mr. Mclntyre and his family.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT,

MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday evening Mr. BRYCE, answering a question put by Mr. KIMBER as to the hostile effect on British trade of the fiscal policy of certain foreign Governments, said Her Majesty's Govern- ment believed that the policy of Free Trade followed by this country was, so far as their own action was con- cerned, the policy best calculated to promote in the future, as it had promoted in the past, British trading interests throughout the world. Mr. MORLEY brought in a bill, which was read the first time, to make temporary provision for the relief ot distress in Ireland.

===== 0002601_18950323_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY._

In the House of Commons on Tuesday Sir WILLIAM HAitcounr, in reply to a question by Mr. W. 01lame, stated that the Customs authorities had no information that a large importation took place into British ports of margarine and other substances purporting to be butter. It any information was furnished as to such importation they would be prepared to take action in the matter. Mr. Warn asked whether, in view of the many deaths caused by the bursting of kitchen boilers during the recent frost, the PRESIDENT of the BOARD Of TRADE would introduce a bill so to amend the Boiler Explosions Act of 1890 as to make it refer to boilers used tor domestic pur- poses exclusively. Mr. BRYCE replied that it was not clear how far a Government inquiry into accidents in private houses would be atteneed with public advan- tage. He was, however, considering whether some means might not be adopted of warning householders of the steps to be taken to prevent such accidents. Asked by Mr. BAYLEY if he was a warethat an address to the Government had been signed by 100 members of Parliament for the appointment of a S!lect Committee to inquire into the arrangements for saving life upon the coasts of the United Kingdom, Sir W. flAucorram said he understood Mr. BAYLEY to mean that the service of life- boats now so admirably discharged by the Lifeboat In- stitution should be taken over at the public cost. That was a very serious question. So many fresh demands were made for State aid in all directions that before he could ask ties House to appoint a Committee he must receive further information as to the grounds for th. inquiry. When the House went into Committee of Supply a discussion took place on the supplementary :v0'.13 of £29,000 grant in aid for the island of Cyprus. Mr. LABOUCHERE, who expreseed the opinion that the annex- ation of Byprus was one et the" most infamous pieces of trickery that ever took place," moved to reduce the vote by £lOO as a protest against nothing being done from year to year, by negotiations with the 'Turkish Government, to remove the indebtedness. Mr. BALFOUR said he could not understand who was injured by the present arrangement. The British taxpayers were not injured, and the Cypriotes were not injured, and those were the only two bodies of persons whom we need trouble ourselves about. Until we knew what develop- ments the Eastern Question was going to take, he would be a rash prophet and a reckless politician who would say that Cyprus might not yet prove to be of great ina- portance. Sir WILLIAM IlAncoura said he had never proposed the abandonment at Cyprus. Under etisting circumstances we could not retire from the obligations into which we had entered. l'he amendment was even- tually withdrawn, and the vote was agreed to by 246 to 20.

WEDNESDAY. THE APPOINTMENT OF MAGISTRATES.

In the House of Commons on Wednesday Mr. T. M. HEALY moved the second reading of the Municipal Fran- chise (Ireland) Bill. The measure was first introduced, he said, more than twenty years ago by Mr. BUTT and others. All those gentlemen had passed away, and the fact remained that though many changes had been made in the English municipal franchise, municipal reform in Irelaud was not one whit more forward, although both the Irish parties had attempted to procure reform. The , central idea of the bill was that every ratepayer sbAuld be able to vote in an Irish borough or municipality, just as anEngliskman was able to do so in an English town. The bill was read a second time. Mr. HEALY then moved that the bill showld be referred to the Grand Committee on Law and this was agreed to. Mr. LUTTRELL then moved the second read- ing of the Justices of the Peace Bill. He said he proposed in his bill that the county councils should make the nominations to the Lord Chan- cellor, and in order that the county councils should be kept in touch with the wants of the district be proposed to allow the chairmen of the district councils to send up names to the county councils, which should be consi- dered by the county councils before the appointments were made. Mr. A. WILLIAMS seconded the motion. Mr. T. Levrsoty mooed the rejection of the bill, and said he thought that to have a committee of the county council who were to nominate the magistrates was the worst plan that could possibly be devised. Sir W. HAR- COURT said he could see nothing in the bill to object to, and he was expressing a far more valuable ophaion than his own when he said the LORD CHANCELLOR was in favour of it. He, on the part of the Government,would give a hearty support to the second reading. Mr. BAL.. FOUR said that, disguise the matter as they liked,if they passed the bill in its present shape, eveh without the 13th clause, they would to all intents and purposes be giving a Parliamentary direction to the Lord Chancel- lor of the day to take the magistrates suggested to him by the borough and the county coune,ils. On a division the second reading was carried by 201 to 163.
