MESSRS. PEARSON AND KNOWLES AND THEIR WORKMEN.

===== 0002601_18950323_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
INTERESTING CORRESPONDENCE.

We have received the following letter :

Sir,—We shall be much obliged if you can find room in your next issue to publish the correspondence, copy of which is enclosed herewith, which has recently appeared in the " Wigan Observer " relating .to the closing 3f our Ince Iron works. The animus which it displays calls for no comment.—Faithfully yours,

TEIE PEARSON AND KNOWLES COAL AND IRON CO. LTD.

15th March 1895.

The correspondence referred to is as follows : THE MOSS SIDE IRONWORKERS' DISPUTE.

Sir,—Kindly allow me space to draw the attention of the miners and other workers in and surrounding Wigan to the terrible dispute which has existed for a consider- able time at the Ince Ironworks. Ido not desire to say a word on the merits of the dispute, as I believe they are well known, but I wish to speak for the men and their families, who, during the late cold winter, must have suffered to a tearful extent from cold and hunger. I know the miners and their cause have no truer friends than the ironworkers of Ice, and I do hope at every pit in the county efforts will be made to take collections, to help these men in their struggle for right. I am infortned that if they can get about three weeks over, their pros- pects will be much brighter. Do help them by your pennies at once. Their cause is your cause. You know what fighting, and hunger, and want mean, because you have felt the same.—Yours obediently, WOODS. House of Commons, Feb. 28th, 1895.

Sir,—My attention has been called to a letter of Mr. S. Woods, M.P., dated February 28th, 1895, which appeared in your issue of March 2nd. It is due to the owners of the works, Messrs. the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Limited, that the facts should be well known. I fear they are not well known, or Mr. Woods would not speak of it as a " terrible dispute." Let the following facts speak for themselves. The whole facts of the case were at the request of the Moss Side ironwcrkers brought before a committee of this board, consisting of the president, vice-president, and two joint secretaries, who attended at Warrington to meet the managing directors of the company in the presence ef two delegates :ram the Moss Side Iron- works and two from Warrington. It was shown to the satisfaction of all present, as appears from the minutes of the meeting, that the cause of closing the Moss Side Ironworks was the extreme depression of trade and the loss of the Canadian, Indian, and Mediterranean orders for the supply of which the works were origin- ally erected. Also that the firm could not provide orders to keep the works at Warrington going, and much less those at Moss Side. At the request of Sir B. Hingley that the Moss Side Ironworkers should participate in the work which was being done at the Warrington works, I am informed that a notice was posted up o that effect, and that in compliance with that arrangement practically full time has been worked at all the Warrington puddling fur- naces owned by the firm, and as a consequence more than half-time, bringing from 203. to 255. per week to the puddler, could have been found for all the Wigan puddlers had they chosen to apply for work, but no ap- plication has been received. That is the statement of the firm as to what has taken place as the result of the suggestion made by Sir B. ilingley. But what lam concerned in is to show that after hearing the explanations made by the managing directors on the 19th December, in the offices at War- rington, everyone present expressed themselves as being satisfied.—l am, sir, yours truly, DANIEL JONES, Employers' Secretary to the Midland Iron and Steel Wages Board. Eilsall, Shifnal, March 6.

The Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., Warrington, Bth March, 1895. Sir,—l beg to draw your attention to a letter of yours that appeared in the "Wigan Observer" on March 2ad, in which you refer to " The terrible dispute which has existed for a considerable time at the Ince Ironworks," and call upon the colliers to assist, as they know what " fighting " means. From a cutting, taken from .th-day's issue of the same paper, which I enclose herewith, you will see that as a matter of fact no " dispute" w:latever exists, and that therefore your letter does our company a serious injustice. I doubt not that with this authori- tive statement of the case before you, the ii.nputation conveyed by your letter will be at once withdrawn as publicly and through the same agency as it was made. _ _ .

Faithfully yours, for the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co. Ltd., JOHN J. BLECKLY, Director.

===== 0002601_18950323_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
S. Woods, Esq., M.P.

Rose Villa, Bryn, Wigan, March 11th, 1835. Dear Sir,—l am in receipt of your letter re the alleged dispute at the Moss Side Ironworks. I deeply regret that you should have entirely misunderstood the object and the aim I had in view in writing the letter to which you refer. First of all I distinctly stated in my letter that I had no intention whatever of entering into the merits of the dispute. In the second place I know there must have been a dispute, or at least some misunder- standing between yourselves and your late employes, as I had a deputation waited upon me at my house on this very subject some five or six weeks ago, in which they made a very full and clear statement of the whole !of the details which led to the stoppage of your works. In the third place the one and only object I had in view in writing my letter was to solicit the aid and pecuniary assistance of the work- ing, classes in and around Wigan for the inainten- ance of these men and their families until such time as they and yourselves had come to some friendly arrange- ment which might lead to the re-starting of the iron- works. I may say that in writing my letter I had not the slightest idea of reflecting on your company, or of saying ene word an the points on which you have had discussion, hut surely I am not to infer from your latter that you object to either myself ar anyone else soliciting aid for the poor,starving men and their familieY,whether the stoppage is in consequence of had trade or in conse- quence of any alleged misunderstanditsg whatever. Thew points, in my judginent, do not arise either in my letter or the letter written by Mr. Daniel Jones to the Wigan " Observer," or in the letter you now write to inc. As you desire publicity in this correspondence, I take the liberty of sendire, both your letter and a copy of this to the' press, which I hope will meet with your approval.—Faithfully yours, S. WOODS. J. J. Bleckly, Esq.

Warrington, 13th March, 1895. S. Woods, Esq., M.P. —Sir, I have to acknowledge the receipt of yours of 11th inst., and in doing so! am bound to say that the iMer- pretation you wish to put upon sour letter to the " Wigan Observer" of March 2nd, is altogether apposed to any reasonable construction of the meaning of the words which you used. The letter " draws attention " ta the terrible dispute at this company's works, and ex- horts the miners to help these men in their struggle for right, " because they know what fighting is." If this means anything, it means that this company is attempt- ing to perpetrate some wrong on these men, and so con- veys an injurious imputation noon it, if the case is not as you represent it. Surely this is no mere appeal for assistance on charitable grounds, as you now assert, hut a summons to contribute to a fund for fighting purposes, altogether unjustifiable under the circumstances. At the time your original letter was written you were probably under the impression that it correctly des- cribed the position, but with the deliberate verdict which was before you when you last wrote of an inde- pendent tribunal, altogether above suspicion, such as the Midland Iron Trades,Wages Board, there could no longer be any doubt about the facts, and considerations of common fairness should have led you to rectify the injustice which your first letter had done. The directors of the company sympathise as deeply as anyone with the distress, which has resulted from the unavoidable 'closing of their Ince works, and in order to alleviate it, so far as it was in their power to do so, made special arrangements at Warrington for the purpose of assisting the Wigan man, but tliese arramtemtmts have been rend -red altogether fruitless, and the distress consequently " aggravated," by the persistent efforts which have been mado,vith a perverse• ?less which is inconceivable, to represent, As a opears in your own letter, that soma dispute or lock-out exi't',- although the allegation has been disproved and ad- mitted to he unfounded by the men's own delegates and representatives. As you say you are forwarding my letter of the 8'..11 inst., and your reply to the papers, I take the liberty of doing the same with this rejoinder, which I regret you should have rendered necessary.— Faithfully yours, For the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company. Limited, (Signed) JOHN J. BLECRLY, Director.

While wanderin7 in the WoGas at Erkin2ton, in Derbyshire, on Saturday, a number of children came acroqs a deep pool which had still a thin caating of Me. Sir,—Will you be so kind as to insert this in your valuable paper, as we deem it our pleasing duty to ex- press our hearttelt thanks to Mr. Alderman Wright and Mr. W. Owen, J.P., for the kind and benevolent manner in which they dealt out help to us during the late frost —also to the whole of the master builders for their kind- ness ? Ile treated upon the sabject of the late epidemic in a very light manner in- deed, referring only to the fact that Mr. J. G. Gornall's report on the matter, was commendable and gave fuller particulars than other documents of its kind. The Chairman invited any person, to come forward and pre- sent written questions. I presented two very important questions, which were very imperfectly read out to the meeting. The game renaarks apply to Cockhedge Mills and other works in the town. ,2nd. liow many children under 10 years of age 44 small-pox in Warrington, during the late epidenc, vaAcinated and unvaccinated. _I will now add a few more How was small-pox first introduced bete in tg.,9g f,icptain the cause of its rapid progress and its extermination,. JOHN MOORFIELD. 44, Parker-street, Warrington, March 14, nss. -- SOCIALISM MEANS THE WORLD FOR THE WORKERS.

" Hord sol't qui mal y pence." Sir,—After very carefully viadiog through what pur- ports to be a letter from Tony Lumpkin in your last issue,l must say that I found in it a deal of coarse abuse without a single word of sound argument adduced. I do not propose to resort to Billingsgate language and mud-throwing, but I am open to argue every point of' the Social Democratic Federation programme through the Fress or on the platform with and against all comers, Tony Lumpkin included. Individualism has bad an innings,has been tried and found wanting. Even Tony Lumpkin, I presume,would confess that all is not well With the present state of affairs the world over. A remedy to the existing evils is what is wanted, and the Socialistic programme it carried out would cure all and every social evil now existing. Why not every honest man in these realms try and help all he can to save the country from the utter ruin and anarchy it is running to ? I wish to cut it short to-day,and as I have no time to write much, I will simply give you some instances of proven facts of robbery perpetrated in our midst in the light of day. The first is an extract from " Justice," Saturday, March 2nd : a The cotton duties in India passed the House of Com- mons by an overwhelming majority after an absurd scare had been got up by the Liberal papers that the Goitermrient was in danger ! We shall ere long have the most serious reasons to consider the economic con- dition of India. As the writer of this note has pointed out for the last eighteen years, the bankruptcy of our great dependency is inevitable. It is now close at hand. More than that, it would have been proclaimed lastyear If a loan of £10,000,0J0 in geld had not been contracted in England, to pay interest, home charges, etc. For our part the sooner forced repudiation comes the bette we shall be pleased. The continuance of the present system which drains the very life-blood out of British India for she sole benefit of interest receivers, officials and ex-officials at home is nothing short of imfamous. A general revolt of the natives in such circumstances would not only be justifiable but highly laudable. The worst of it is all of the men who have been at the head of the Indian Treasury know, and have admitted, in their confidential reports that this awful drain of over £28,000,000 a year reckoned in gold, and now about £50,000,000 reckoned in silver, means and must mean the utter ruin of the country. But they keep silence and pocket their possessions at home. In the face of the catastrophe impending the cotton duties business is a silly affair." Second, last week's" Truth," brought out a very gross abuse about Lord Dudley's mining rights. By an act obtained by one of his ancestors over a hundred years ago, the Earl bas the right of alining over the whole of rensett chase near Boreslev Hill, without liability for damage to property on the surface. The result is that in district after district, houses have been ruined ' • and just lately between thirty and forty people in Quarry Banks have received notice to quit their houses pendin., mining operations, which are bound to bring about their collapse. "There houses be it observed do not belong to Lord Daley, In many instances they are the free- hold property of the actual occupiers and represent the savings of a life-time. Not one ot these poor free-holders, bewever, is entitled to a penny compensation." Third, at the last sitting of the Camberwell Vestry the agenda paper contained a statment by Dr. F. L.Teed,the Public analyst,to the effect that 23 samples of milk taken in the last quarter, 13 or 454 per cent. were adulter- ated. The same authority states in the last annual re- port that the people of Camberwell pay £lOO,OOO per annum fer water in their milk, and ascribes the high rate of infant mortality to low nutrition consequent upon the infants being fed with it. The matter was re- ferred to the Sanitary Committee. All these dishonest proceedings are being carried on all round by the" thrifty individualists "and Tony ap- proves of the system . and is afraid of a change. All we Socialists ask for is " Dieu et notre Droit."—Yours fraternally,

===== 0002601_18950323_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
A. BROUCHEMITE.

recokni

WHERE IS THE CHURCH? Sir,--I gather from the New Testament that unity and brotherly love are essential and distinguishing features of the church founded by Jesus of N.. zareth and his lowly followers. But wkere is such a church to be found

on earth at present? We have many pretenders to the distinction, but where is the genuine article? I cannot recognise it in the pomp3us and inflated institution called the Church of Rome, which places the temporal and spiritual interests of its followers unreservedly in the hands of an Italian Pope.: it le not the half religious, half political Establishment of England whose Bishops swear allegiance to,and holdtheir Sees from, a woman : nor is it fund in the congeries of sects known as Dis- sent. All the various denominations I have referred to differ widely both in doctrine and discipline, and the charity which they show to one another and to the world is of the scantiest dimensions. Known with God collectively are the members of the spiritual body of Christ, scattered as, I believe, they are among all the sects; buta concrete tangible Church of Christ, bound together by the love of their Master, and having one Lord, one faith, and one baptism is a phenomenon not visible to human eyes. Next to the Romish, the Anglican is the sect which proclaims most loudly from the housetops that it is the one and only Church of Christ in the world. Yet,strange to say, appeals are made to every quarter but the right one in support of the allegation. The rites and cere- monies of defunct Judaism, profane history, true and false tradition, our pious ancestors, &c., &c., are all made to do duty as upholders of the Eng- lish Establishment. But surely, if the church is a divine institution it has no need for such rotten pillars as these, but will be able to stand upon its own legs and successfully vindicate its claims in its own proper way. Churchmen know that the Church of Christ was founded by Jesus of Nazareth arid his Apostles nineteen centuries ago, and they are also aware that in the gospels, Acts of the Apostles, and Epistles comprised in the New Testament they have what they, no doubt, regard as divinely inspired re- cords of the lives and teaching of these men concerning both the church and the world. Then why don't they come out boldly arid manfully, and fight the battle for their Church on this ground ? If their Church is the Church of Christ pure and simple,they can have nothing to fear but much to gain by such a course. If the Church defenders in Warrington can show us plainly and con- clusively that the Church is by law established in this country, with its enormous revenues wrung from poor labourers, with its bloated hierarchy called and ordained by Statesmen, and disciples manufactured by prosy at a time when they could not distinguish their right hand from their left, is synonymous with the one established by the labours and sufferings of Him who had not when) to lay his head,Tet it be done at once. The times demand a clear and explicit statement of the case backed up with chapter and verse. Nothing short of this will satisfy the spirit of inquiry that is now sifting the churches., Besides, disestablishenent and disendowment are looming in the near future, and it behoves Churchmen to show a good reason for the faith that is in them. If they can do this the hands of the spoiler may be arrested ; if they cannot,the status, and loaves and fishes of their Zion will be taken away.—Yours truly,

===== 0002601_18950323_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
TON A.

TWO WRONGS; ONE RIGHT? " Do as you would be clone by." Old (anti-socialistic) Maxim. Sir,—The plot thickens. Another budding socialist has ventured to push out his tender petals to face the chill March winds of criticism. Assuming the taking title of "Rosebud," he, not wishing to let "conceal- ment like a worm i'th bud" prevent him from showing the great extent of what he might know of the subject in band, modestly deprecates hie own ability to " broaden Tony Lumpkin's views." Nevertheless I, in return, will endeavour to deepen his superficial views on the subject of land robbery. Socialists of the Rosebud and Brouchemite type are great advocates of broad views. A.B. in last issue dilates upon anything—from cotton duties to milk adulteration—rather than face the question at issue. These good folk would much rather deal with many irrelevancies than calmly and critically examine the subject in hand. The discur- sive and declamatory style has its advantages to the Labour Mis-Leader. It tends to excitement and confus- ion of thought and gives the Socialistic mob an excuse for shouting in place of thinking—which last is indeed a function it abhors—liking neither the work nor they who set them to it. Hence Brouchemiteist discursiveness. Convenient and safe. And now for my budding friend. He has fallen into the common error of judging one age by the standard of morality of another age. The land we call England, I believe, was once held and owned by a people called the Britons. These were disposseaied by the Romans, fol- lowed by the Saxons, Danes and Normans. Now, in the ethics of the time there was no wrong in this. What the Conquerer did to the vanquished so the vanquished would have done to the conqueror had the fates been so minded. The "Right of Conquest" was a right none then disputed—even in theory—and is even new prac- tised. If this be not so what are we doing in Gibraltar, in Africa—in India ? Did the then possessors of these cr.,,lotrits invite us to come over and govern them ? Well, l U call this by Its pr.per name—thieving. So we et S will say that, judged by thd rtnent nf n.-9r* ity, the Land of England hee tfteil stolen—which the Socialists offer as a pretext for stealing it again ! To restore it to its original owners ? By no means ! To divide Socialistically and then to fight ever for the big- gest share! Personally I prefer the fighting to come oil first before the division ! But if the land is to be handed over to anybody "en bloc" surely the AVelsh—as the descendants of the British are the fittest recipients. Now the name Brcucheinite has not a very British ring with it; and possibly the Rosebud may be the product of aeother soil I Again It IA very funny to note the 1364110;i with which these land-litters give theinselees away. Our A.B tells us that" Individualism has been tried and found wanting." . . . Tes,lndividualisin has been tried and the British Empern is ar esult. Socialism has also been tried and pros:Wl miserable failure—wherever tried liesses its merits,: 'A 'Socialist colony could not exist two years tottitent aid from outside individualism. By the wav it Initay be news to some of the "Intelligent readers touchingly appealed to by Rosebud, to hear that I att, a Socialist ! Yes ! I have a system of my own which involves good to all without injustice to any. But Ido not intend to reveal my system until about 50 per cent. of human nature has been eliminated from man and my contemporaries are all of one stature— equal in strength, wisdom, and mental calibre. For then and then only will Mob-Socialism a la Brouchernite and Co..be possible or be anything but a huge political ex- periment foredoomed to failure. When men are machine made and turned out like pins.identical in every respect, Ideal Socialism may then lift up its head and cry " Eureka." In studying the literature of Mob-Socialism, one is more and more impressed with the idea that a new Socialistic decalogue is required. "Thou shalt not steal" (from a Socialist) is quite current now, —eventually, " Thou shalt do no murder " —(on a Socialist) may find a place therein ; —while they are even openly and avowedly coveting their neighbour's goods. It is really wonderful how far our frail nature will carry us in the way of " Doing evil that good may come "—" good " that is to Our- selves and cause—what we imagine to he good. The history of the Church shows how anything that told against it was suppressed, if possible, however good in itself. So the modern preachers of Socialism will let I nothing in the shape of old " moralities " stsnd in the way to hinder the advent of the political millennium. Meanwhile let Rosebud and A.B. get out of their clothes as quickly as possible for undoubtedly, somo of the materials have been stolen at some time or other,ancl did they pay for them with money quite clear of this disqualifying reproach ? Land natioualization has not an honest leg to stand on—so it limps on Socialistic crutches. —Yours, &c.,

TONY LUMPKIN.

THE TRADE DEPRESSION.

------ Sir,—Mt. Bryce, the President of the Board of Trade. wishes u. to believe that the trade of this country speedily improge. If the majority of the directors Of the large ironworks of this town were also of that opinion we might reasonably hope for some improve- ment, but I do not think they are. Mr. Bryce stated that since 1846 the periods of trade depression had been in no instance longer than six years, and as the present depression had lasted for about five years there would now probably be a change for the better. I venture to think that the trade of this country has been had m-re than five years, and if in the past it has nearly always improved after six years' depression it does not follow that it will do so now, for the causes of depressed trade in this country are different from tiv,se which existed thirty years ago,and the President of the hoard of Trade ought to know and acknowledge it, for unless we rtalise our position and meet it with manly honesty and courage I fear that we may go from bid to worse. I believe that some firms in the iron trade are and have been busy making machinery for other countries, and men and women will work at that inachinery,sometimes at low wages, and manufacture goods to be imported into this country and to swell the number of the unem- ployed. I cannot now go further into this subject, but hope this country will not much longer he, by pleasing hopes of better trade, betrayed.--Yours, S. KENYON. Warrington, March 18, 1895.

After stating that his first connection was 1 with the ' Baptist Ckurch in Yorkshire, and giving a \relation of his return to Methodism and his three years at Didsbury College, he said in 1893 he was appointed connexional evangelist. He had not a very good start there as he was bitterly opposed by the publicans and their friends. - He then related one or two amusing ' anecdotes, and stated that before his mission was ended he filled the chapel and saved 137 souls. His next place wai Uttoxeter, where he had at first- fearful pre- I iudice to meet. It was a very drunken place, but the first two persons who knelt at the communion rail the first Sunday were a brewer and his daughter. He then proceeded to give an account of his mission work at the various places he had visited, and as his recitals were smartened with a keen vein of humour, the audience thoroughly enjoyed his discourse. Of Horncastle he spoke in scathing terms, characterising it as a perfect hornet's next, and said he was glad when he had shaken ' the dust of the place from his feet. .In conclusion he said up and down the country they were telling the old story, and God was blessing their work, and it was the fund for carrying on this work they were asked to con- tribute to that night. DEATH OF Mn. JOHN RODGERS. WARGRAVE. We have this week to record with regret the death of Mr. John Rodgers of Wargrave-road,Earlee- town, which took place at his residence on Thurs- day morning at the age of 47 years. The deceased was a foreman blacksmith at the Vulcan Foundry, and about three weeks ago suffered from an attack of influenza. From this he apparently recovered, and resumed his employment. On Wednesday, in last week however he had to cease work, owing to not feeling well, but serious results were not anti- ' cipated. On Thursday he had to take to his bed, and except at intervals lie was unable to leave it. Dr. Valentine was called in, but as he did not im- prove he obtained on Tuesday evening the assig- nee of Dr. Starkey Smith of Warrington, and an examination showed that Mr. Rodgers was suffer- ing from cerebral hemorrhage, and no hopes were entertained of his recovery. He rapidly grew worse and on Wednesday lapsed into unconsciousness,and jjieer:y Pnraday Merning. The deCeeeed is not an old resident, lieving lived here only a little over three years. He was a Scotchman and came to England some five years ago to take up a position with Messrs. Peacock of Gorton, and from there came to Vulcan to take up the appointment Vitiell he held until his death, lie WAS greatly reipeeted by the workmen under his charge, and also by the , circle ef friends he had made during his short reAdolce. Lie Waa An advanced Liberal in politics, and interested himself eery much during the recent elections to the District Council, having presided at one or two meetings in the Wargrare Ward. He leaves a widow and five sons and daughters. The interment will take place on Saturday at Sighthill Cemetery, Glasgow. THE "MESSIAH' AT EARLESTOWN. A representation of I i anders oratorio "Messiah " was given in the Town Hall, Esrlestown, on Thurs- day evening, by the Earlestown Musical Society, before a fair audience. The society hes not been in existence more than six months, and they deserve the greatest credit for displaying sufficient courage in undertaking such a difficult task. In addition to a band and chorus of GO performers, efficient principals had been engeged, viz., soprano, Miss Young (Sir Charles Italic's Concerts, &c), alto, Miss Whitliam (Manchester Vocal Society), tenor, Mr. H. Berry (Liverpool Concerts, &c.), bass, Mr. J. 11, Thomas (Birkenhead). The leader of the band was Mr. Peplow, Miss E. L. Greenall presided at the piano, Mr. E. C. Pryor, organ, and Mr. E. Leyland conducted. To go int o detail over each chorus and recitation would take up more time' than we have at disposal, we might, however, say that in the compositiou of the choir, the bass was rather heavy, and at times com- pletely drowned the altos. On the whole the choruses were exceedingly well rendered, although as only natural, several slight errors crept in. but they were nothing of any moment. Mr. Berry handled the parts set down for him in a masterly manner, his singing of' Comfort ye," being excep- tionally good. Miss Young was in tine voice, and gave a clear exposition of the various recits which she sang, especially " There were Shepherds,"" And the Angel said."" And Suddenly." Miss iarbitham had some difficult tasks, but she performed them with credit, and Mr. Thomas's efforts were received with evident satisfaction. Mr. Leyland conducted with ability, and the orchestral accompaniments were with one or two exceptions,excellently played Miss Greenall and Mr. Pryor executed their tasks with every credit.

CONFERENCE OF ODDFELLOWS AT EARLESTOWN.

A meeting of Oddfellows, under the auspices of the Loyal Viaduct Lodge, E irlestown, was held at the lodge room, Wellington Hotel, Earlestown, on Saturday, to . .

discuss the question of " The iusolvencv of lodges and district." Y.P.G.N. T. Tunstall presided, and amongst

those present were :—P.P.G.N. -Wilkins, of Liverpool (secretary of the Lancashire Conference), Bro. Brockle- bank, of St. Helens, Bro. Cowie, of St. Helens, Bro. M. Battersby,District Secretary,and others. The Chairman said they had met to hear something about the insol- vency of lodges and district. It was an important ques- . . .

tion, aud one that Oddfellows should hear more about. The object of the meeting was to try and enlighten their members as far as they possibly could, and show the exact position their lodge stood in. P.P.G.N. Wilkins said the question was, what consti- tuted solvency and insolvency, and what were the ob- vious remedies for insolvency where it existed, and the best means of keeping up solvency where it existed? He was glad that the brethren of that lodge had taken to heart sotne of the lessons that had been taught by the conference at Bolton. That conference was not called to find fault with the various districts in the Unity,only

;,3 tar as showed they were not open to argummit, but was called to ask the prominent members in every dis-

_ trict to attend there and discuss mattersiot:benefit to all. But they thought by calling the lodges together they could raise discussion and lead to the truth coming out, and the truth would lead to a better state of things at the next valuation. He thought no one who considered for a moment what a Friendly Society was, would deny the imp.rtance of that Society being solvent, and per- haps it would be as well to commence that discussion by explaining what was meant by solvency, as there might be a deal of uncertainity on the point. It had been stated at times that every lodge was solvent when it had £lO per member. They knew perfectly well that there were plenty of lodges in the Liverpool district that had £lO per member, which were perfectly retten. He took it that the solvency of a lodge was recognised just the same as solvency in business. Some lodges simply took their balance sheet, and said they had received so many contributions, this year, and had only paid so much sick pay, and were so many pounds better off than

last year. But they forgot that during that time their lodge, consisting of 300 or 400 members had increased its age one year, and if they neglected to provide for that Liability, they would find that their balance was not so great as it formerly was, and instead of getting solvent and saving money year by year. they were not saving money against that liability. Alodge was solvent when the money they expected to obtain was greater in amount than the claims they expected to obtain. The practice or method by which that was obtained was what they called a valuation, and that valuation was a new thing so far as Friendly Societies were concerned. He then proceeded to enter into detail in order to show the principle on which the valuation was worked. In order to get at a valuation they wanted to get really at the value of a member's contributions. Taking an hypothetical case of the valuation of a lodge showing a deficiency of £lOOO, and if the lodge went on its natural course, and they did not admit any more members into their lodge eenleene Would be short of £1,003 in sick - pay, and funeral benefits, altogether but that only took • place when the whole Ot the capital was exhausted. Tie; elotglree of adequate contributions was an import- nc ; it meant a proper amount of contributions for the benefit promised. It was clearly laid down in the text books, that it was impossible for any • lodge to err from ignorance on that point. Another important point was the regular investment of funds. He believed the regular and mompt investment of lodges in the past had more to do with the present state of lodges than any other cause that could be pointed out. If they had seen the balance sheets of many lodges they would find it to be a common practice to. have £2OO or £3OO in the Post Office Saving Bank, and the only advantage was that it was safe, but when they had said that, they had Aaid all they could say in favour of it. There was an- other worse investment, and that was money left in [ treasurer's hands, by which they were not fulfilling their appointed duty, and that was the duty of I earning interest. The rule of keeping the , funds separate, was supposed to be a ' primary one in connection with their Society at the present ' time, but they were also aware it was very seldom car- ried out; as, although the funds are supposed to be kept eeparate, yet they often feund on the balance sheet, due to the treasurer £5O or £lOO. It was however not due to the treasurer, but to the sick and funeral fund. With regard to the spending of the money, there were just a few points that might be mentioned. They found that sometimes out of charity, sick pay was paid when it should not be paid. There were a good many questions raised as to what were proper investments, at the pre- sent time. It was clearly laid down in the Act of Par- 'lament as to how far they could go in investing their money. They could invest perhaps best of all on pro- perty, and that was where they would get the best 'amount of interest, especially if the Committee kept a ' watchful eye over it. It was to the interest of the .younger members of the lodge tro see that the funds were properly invested. Solvency in lodges was to be secured by proper contributions, proper benefits, and proper in- vestments of funds. If those three principles were car- ried out, lie did not think they could find a lodge that was insolvent. With regard to insolvency of districts, the districts were only the amalgamation of the lodges, and if in their own individual lodges they could work to make their lodge selve:nt, there would not be much trouble with the districts. (Applause.) Br ~13tick'ebenk agreed with the previous speaker that if they had the lodges sound and safe, the district would also be so. After commenting upon the efficiency of the management of the Loyal Viaduct lodge, he said the only thing that he could see for the future prosperity of the lodge, was to accommodate their continuous sick pay to the requirements of their capital. By decreasing their continuous sick pay by 20 per cent., they might hope at the next valuation to be sound and solvent, possibly with a small surplus, but otherwise their deficiency would surely increase. (Hear, hear.) Bro. D. Williams, after referring to the remarks of the previous speakers, gave a lot of figures referring to ttfe question of deficiency, and also quoted statistics show.' ug the expenditure per member in sick benefits for ten years, stating that duhng the last four years it had averaged over 15s. per member for that particular lodge. , Bro. W. Gilmour said the lodge had for 30 years en- deavoured to lead the district in a straight and proper manner. A man juiced a Friendly Society with the view that when he got old there would be something for him, instead of going to the workhouse. He was a strong advocate against reducing the benefits of old members. fie should never consent to the reduction of the sick pay to old members ; his experience was that they were paying inure to the young members than to the old. (Hear, hear.) Bro. Cowie, St. Helens, also contributed some general remarks on the subj ct-. —Bro. M. Battersby, district secretary, expressed his pleasure at the meeting which he hoped would be of some benefit, and Bro. Silcock, Colborne, having spoken, Br.-. Connor proposed a vote of thanks to the visitors, and Bro. Gilmour having seconded it was ueanimously accorded, and the busi- ness terminated.

WESLEYAN HOME MISSIONS.

TEIE ANNUAL MEETING. The Rev. T. Ogden Taylor preached sermons, morning and evening, on Sunday, at St. Paul's Wesleyan Chapel, Runcorn, in behalf of the home minion work et the

Wesleyan denomination. Thera were large congrega- tions. At the evening service the choir sang" Send out thy light " (Gounod). Collections were taken in aid of

horne Mission work.

The annual tea meeting ,was held on Thursday even-

log in St. Paul's Churck.- There was a good attendance. I Mr. G. Bellamy occupied the chair, and there were also present :—Revs. T. Ogden Taylor. J. S. Hughes, T. Brackeubury, W. George, J. H. 'rite, and Messrs. P. Speakman,J.P., C.C., F. J. Norman,J.., A. R. Norman, E. Taylor, and R. Robinson. The Rev. W. George read the annual report. Them- coma for the year ending at conference, 1894, including £l,OOO appropriated for the reserve fund was £35,523 2s. ea. The expenditure during the same period had been £36,534 15s. 4d., leaving a deficiency of over £l,OOO. During the year Runcorn had contributed £74 17s. 4d. One hundred and nine home missionaries were reported as engaged in the Methodist home mission work, as against 100 last year and N the year before. The number of ministers appointed for work in the army and Royal nary was twelve. The Chairman in the course of a short address said as a church they came inte existence to carry on home mission work and it was by carrying on this work that they justified their existence. (Applause.) Referring to the social evils of the present day, lie said the onty solution of them was to be found in the people becoming possessed with the spirit of the Lord Jesus Christ. (Applause.)

The Rev. Ogden Taylor then delivered an address c n the work of the Wesleyan Home Missionary society in the counties and cities. FL: raid the political power ( f the present day had passed into the hands of the work- ingmen, and it was of the greatest importance that they should be brought up in the lines of sound Christianity. He believed the righteousness of the Bible was the right- eousness which exhalted a nation and if a nation cast itself from the Bible it would be a bad day for the • nation. He hail been a close and sym- pathetic observer of the artizans of this country. He had tried to gain their friendship and did n(l5 believe that they were unbelievers in religion. In Lan- cashire, Cheshire, and Yorkshire the working people still retained much of the religious teaching gained in the Sunday school in their earlier days and they only wanted genuine sympathy to bring ti.ein over to the churches. It was his opinion that in the future, the Methodist church would be in the closest sympathy with the great masses of the people of this country. The Rev. J. S. Harris, of Irwell-st. mission, Salford, gave an address on the work of his mission in the slums of Salford. He said the mission was commenced about three years since. The mission room was one of the old sanctuaries once amidst a huge population, but which had now deserted the neighbourhood and re- moved to the suburbs. The place was nearly empty and a new departure was inevitable. The rev. gen- tleman explained that three years ago he was sent spe- cially by the conference, with a free hand, to again fill this old desertedchapel. He referred to his methods of visitation, of advertisersent, of making use of the Press, whicn, he stated, had often enabled him to reach a larger congregation outside than he reached Inside. Open air services were held, and these were helped by means of a brass baud. Be spoke ot the co-operation lie re- ceived from the laymen who still attended the chapel, in his work amengst the slums of Salford. The mission had been a suecese from the first. There had been four- teen quarterly meetings and increases in membership had been reported at every meeting. The two great watchwords of their mission, and of all the Wesleyan cnissiona were, "Concentration, Adaptation." He re- ferred to the liquor traffic, which was their deadliest enetny,and had to be fought resolutely. The great ques- tion of the unemployed had forced itself upon their at- tention. He bad found work for a number of men,and he believed this was the truest kindness. file rejoiced , that the social question was coming to the from, and declared that at the bottom of every social problem there was a social wrong. ,Mr. P. Speakman proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman, and in doing so said home mission work had his heartiest 'empathy. He spoke of some of the die- graceful sights he had seen in the poorer districts,which were a discredit to a civilised country. fie also thought they could do with more mission work in Runcorn.—Mr. T. Simister seconded and the motion was carried. The Chairman replied, and the meeting terminated.

IMPERIAL PARLIMENT. (Continued from page 6.) THURSDAY. WELSH DISESTABLISFIMENT. In the House of Commons on Thursday Mr. BRYCE re- plying to Mr. BOWLES, said the official receiver in the case of the" Balfour group "of companies had borrowed large sums at the express desire of the committees of in- spection and after the proposals had been approved by the Court. Sir E. GREY stated, in answer to Mr. BOWLES, that during the hostilities between France and Madagascar no part of British territory would be allowed to be used as a base for hostile operations. Asked by Sir E. A. BARTLETT whether the Swazis had agreed to accept Boer administration, and whether any Boer armed force had entered Swaziland, Mr. BUXTON said he believed the Swazi had peacefully accepted the administration of the South Africa Republic. In answer to Mr. SCHWANN, Sir E. GREY stated that cases of ill-treatment of Armenians which had been brought to the notice of the Government by the reports ot British Consular officers had already formed the sub- ject of separate representations to the Porte. The Government would spare no effurt to promote the effec- tive co-operation of other Powers to secure the introduc- tion of reforms in the government of Armenia. Mr. ASQUITH, in moving the second reading of the Es- tablished Church (Wales) Bill, said the object of the bill was to terminate the legal establishment of the Church of England in Wales. That was its central and essential provision, and everything else was subsidiary to that main and governing purpose. It ws.s a historical fallacy to represent the Church of England as ever baying been a mere offshoot and dependency of the Church of Rome. He defied anyone to dispute the proposition that whether or not there was a transfer of the privileges and endow- ments of one Church to another. Parliament did,between 1530 and 1570, assert and exercise the right of prescrib- ing the auditions of tenure on which every ecclesiastical benefice in this country was held. That was the price which a Natioaal Church paid fGr the so-called privilege of establishment. It was for them to consider whether : the privileges and disabilities were worth the ' price which they had always had to pay and would always have to pay for that state of things. Sir M. H. Bitecn contended that the Government were attempting to attack the Church under the disguise of yielding to what they called the national demands of Wales. The Principality was to be treated in this matter as a separate nationality, contrary to history, contrary to geographical facts, and he believed not in accordance with any views which the Government would be prepared to carry to their legitimate conclu- sions. The debate was continued by Mr. GERBER/. RoBERTS, Mr. PENROSE FITZGERALD, Mr. T. P. PRICE, Mr. U. LODER, Lord CR.INBORNE, and other members, and then adjourned.

In the House of Lords Lord HOBROUSB moved the second reading of a bill to amend an Act passed in 1781 for preventing certain abuses and profanities on Sundays. Ele stated that the recent prosecution of some people at Leeds who had wished to provide rational instruction and amusement on Sundays had led to the introduction of the bill, which provided that if lectures or addresses were delivered with a view to the public good, and not by way of trade or for the pecuniary profit of the promoters, those persons should not be liable to prosecution under the old Act of GEORGE 111. The Archbishop of CANTERBURY said the bill would require an enormous amount of re- casting in Committee, and if it was read a second time he would suggest that it should be referred to a Select Committee. Lord Caoss Suggested that the second read- ing should be adjourned sine die, and that a Committee should be appointed to consider in what way the Act of • Emma 111. could best be amended. Lord Ilontrousa agreed to this course, and the second reading was ad- journed.

REDUCTION IN THE PRICE OF GAS.—The Frodsham Gas and Water Company have decided that a reduction of 3d. per 1.000 cubic feet will be allowed on all gas con- sumed by meter (prepayment meters excepted) if the accounts are paid when delivered or within month 1 from date of invoice, commencing with the March quarter. LONG SERVICE MF.DALS FOR VOLUNTEERS.—At the presentation of long service medals at Chester, on Wed- nesday, the following Volunteers at Frodsham, who are members of the "I" company, 2nd Earl of Chester regiment, received medals :—Lance-Sergeant John Wilkinson, Private James Holland, and Private James Caldwell. wEst.v.ran 'lons Misstoxs.—A meeting was held in Trinity Wesleyan Chapel, Frodsham, on Wednesday evening, in support of Wesleyan home missions. WARRINGTON.

P.S.A. AT THE Y.M.C.A.

On Sunday afternoon Mr. John Taylor took the chair at the usual meeting of the above. The Rev. J. Yonge was the speaker and he gave a very inter- esting address on "Religious Manliness." Mr. J. H. Draper acted as soloist and sang with good effect the solos "Comfort ye" (Handel) and " There is a Green Hill." Mr. F. Hickman acted as accompanist and Messrs. 'Tendon and Gregson assisted in the meeting which was well attended. ACCIDENT AT MESSRS. CROSPIELD'S SOAP WORKS.

A workman named John Beswick, 44 years of age, and residing at 47, Winwick-road,, met with a serious accident on Monday afternoon whilst fol- lowing his employment at the above manufactory. Ile seems to have been working at a consideraole height from the ground when, by some accident, as yet unexplained, he was precipitated to the earth. He was picked up and taken to the Infirmary, where, after being medically examined, it was found that he had sustained a scalp wound and a contusion of the back. Ile was detained in the institution.

Mn. E. L. FLETCHER. The "London Gazette" contains the announce- ment that Mr. E. L.' Fletcher, of Warrington, has been promoted to the rank of second-lieutenant in the Ist Batt. Prince of Wales' Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment)—in other words, in the Warrington Volunteer Corps. In the "Picture Magazine" for March is a reproduction of two photographs, one by day and the other by night, of a couple of snow figures which Mr. Fletcher had photographed and sent up to that paper. The photo- graph taken at night was accomplished with the aid of a magnesium flash light. SEWIN4 Ma.cßusEs, Hand £2 18e. 6d. Treadle £4 4s. witb handsome walnut cover.—J. and W. Dur Tax, 16 and ld

Sankey-. • Wwrringten.—A VISIT OF MRS. COMMISSIONER BOOTH-TUCSER TO WARRINGTON. Special services in connection with the Salvation Army were held in the Co-operative Hall, Winwick- street, on Sunday. There were about 7CO present, among whom may be mentioned :—Mrs. Commis- sioner Booth-Tucker, and Field Commissioner Miss Booth, who were assisted by the General's private secretary (Major Milan), Major and Mrs. Howe, of the Liverpool Province, and Major Alice Lewis, of the military and naval league. Mrs. Booth-Tucker took for her text,trom the words, "Then said I,woe is me for I am undone because 1 am a man of un- clean lips." At the close of the service a collection was made amounting to about .£8 13s. SUPPOSED SUICIDE AT WINWICX. On Wednesday morning about six o'clock, the body of an old man, named John Clare, was found hanging in an outhouse at Hultne, near Winwick. The deceased was 62 years of age,and for about two years had been a greengrocer. During the last few months, he had frequently complained of pains in his head. On Monday night last he complained of being unwell whilst in bed, but when his wife awoke about six o'clock on Tuesday morning she found that he had got up. He as not seen during all that day and night. On the next morning a search was instituted by two young men, who saw his body in an outhouse ;some distance off. They informed P.C. Groarke of the affair,and he went and

. . cut the body down, life being quite extinct. The rope employed was a clothes line, and the feet of the deceased were within three inches off the ground. SEWING MACHINES, Haul £2 18s. 6d. Treadle £4 4e. with handsome walnut cev.r.—J. and W. DUTTON, 16 and 13, Sankey-it.. Warrington —Advt. TEMPERANCE MEETING AT PADGATE. _ . . .

On Tuesday evening a meeting of the British Women's Temperance Association was held in the Padgate Wesleyan Schoolroom. Miss Bradbury, of Warrington, occupied the chair, and was supported on the platform by Mrs. Yonge (president), Mrs, Garnett (Peuketh), Miss Emily Bennett, and Mrs. Gunnell. Tnere was a good attendance of ladies, and a scattering of gentlemen. Mrs. Garnett pro- posed the following resolution :—" That this meet- ing, while grateful to Lord Rotebery and Sir W. Harcourt, for the prominent place given to the Liquor Traffic (Local Control) Bill in the Queen's speech, respectfully urges the Government to use their utmost endeavour to pass the Bill this session through all its stages in the House of Commons," and that copies of this resolution be sent to Lord Kosebery, Sir William Harcourt, and the local mem- ber. Mrs. Yonge, wife of the Rev. John Yonge, seconded the resolution and in a neat speech advo- cated the cause. The resolution was unanimously ' adopted. Miss Bennett sang a sole during the course of the meeting. BAPTIST MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT SOCIETY. At the usual meeting of the' Baptist Mutual Im- provement Society,held in the clioolrootn,Golhorne- st.,on Friday evening, hiss Gemmell gave n address on" Woman." The essayist alluded to the efforts women were now making to obtain more public spheres of labour, and rema:ked that this aro e from men having usurped positions that women were better able to fill. She agprecated men serving as shop assistants, as she tuought women by their superior taste, judgment and disposition were better qualified to attend upon the ladies who formed the majority of buyers. Still, she was no advocate for women to occupy these positions. She believed woman's true sphere was the home, and it was there that, by her ready tact, her loving sym- pathy, and her tender affection, she best fulfilled the duties she was destined to till. Au interesting discussion ensued, the speakers being Messrs. N. Balmford, R. Miller, S. Rickers, 11. Walker, W. Patrick, and John S. Green (chairmsn). Votes of thanks concluded the meeting. WEsLEYAN CIRCUIT QUARTERLY MEETING.

The quarterly meeting of the Warrington Wesleyan Circuit was held on Monday afternoon, when tea was provided by the circuit stewards in the band room of Bold-st. Wesleyan Church. At the subsequent meeting, the Rev. J. Kirtlan pre- sided, and was supported by the Revs. Owen Wat- kins, G. D. Mason. and J. Gunnell, the circuit stewards (Messrs. W. Bennett and R. G. Henshall), and a large number of the trustees, local preachers, and stewards of the various churches in the circuit. The numerical statement presented by the Chairman showed that there were 1,057 members, with 53 on trial, being an increase on the quarter of eleven,and of 22 on the year. There were also 271 junior mew- -1 bers. The thanks of the meeting were presented to the Revs. Owen Watkins and G. D. Mason, the former of whom was unanimously invited to become th 3 6uperhiteudent of the circuit in Sep- tember, and the latter, second minister. The Rev. W. G. Triggs of Redhill, was unanimously invited as the third minister at the next conference. The Rev. W. Potts of Nottingham, was invited to be the superintendent at the conference of 1896, and the Rev. G. Ha^rbottle, of Blackpool, as the second minister. The report of the new church at Latch- ford was given, from which it appeared that only £l5O was required to secure the connexional grant. Arrangements were made for the Liverpool Synod which is to be held in Warrington in May. This will be attended by the circuit stewards ex efficio, and also by Miss Bessie Dutton and Dr. T. Starkey Smith, J.P., who were elected to represent the cir- cuit. A memorial was signed by the Chairman on behalf of the meeting, against the African slave trade, and was ordered to be forwarded to the prime minister. CLAIM AGAINST A WIDNES MILKDEALER. At Warrington County Court, on Thursday, Her- bert Lees, of Dairy Farm, Newton-by-Daresbury, sued Thomas Parry, milk dealer, of Sitnm's Cross, Widnes, for £l6 17s. 10d., balance owing for milk supplied and for the value of six tankards detained by defendant. Mr. Arthur :Browne appeared on behalf of the plaintiff and informed his Honour that at the last court day the defendant had dis- puted the amount and the case had been adjourned in consequence, but as he now failed to attend he (Mr. Browne) asked for judgment for the amount claimed. The case was that the defendant had been supplied with milk by plaintiff from November 16th, 1893, to December 15th, 1893 to the amount of £34 17s. 10.1. The defendant had also detained six tankards which had never been returned, and which plaintiff valued at .£6 making a gross total of £4O 17s. 10c1. In January 1894, plaintiff placed the matter in the hands of Messrs. Mainwaring for collection, and they wrote to the defendant, de- manding payment of the same. In response to the letter, the defendant attinded at the office of Messrs. Mainwaring, and there met the plaintiff. The latter asked him if he disputed the claim,and he replied that he did not, and only wanted time to pay the money. It was therefore arranged that deferment shoula pay £5 down and .£1 per week afterwards. He only paid .£23 until the entry of the summons into the County Court of February 25th. Since that day, he had paid .£1 leaving a balance of £l6 17s. 10d. which included £6 for tank- ards. Mr. lirowne's statement was borne out by the plaintiff, and George McGowan, clerk in the employ of Messrs. Mainwaring. His Honour gave judgment for the plaintiff for ..£lO 17s. 10d., and £6 damages, such amount to reduced to £lO 17s. 10d. and 10s. for the detention of the tankards, should they be returned within a week.

NOTIOE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY OF CHESTER will be held at the COURT HOUSE, NETHER KNUTSFORD, in and for the said County, on MONDAY, the Bth day of April next, at half- past eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Bucklow. Macclesfield, and Northwich, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials af all prisoners com- mitted from such Hundreds, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knuts- ford District must be in attendance at the Court House, Knutsford, at the opening of the Court on MONDAY, the Bth day of April next, at half-past eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE Court will be Adjourned to the Castle of Chester, on TatrasnAv, the 11th day of April next, at eleven e'clock in the Forenoon. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Broxton, Eddisbury, Nantwich, and Wirral, forming the Chester District,and the trials of all prisoners committed from the last-men- tioned Hundreds will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester. at the opening of the Court on THURSO& Y, the 11th day of April next, at eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester. Chester, 18th March, 1895.

PRUDENTIAL ASSURANCE COMPANY LIMITBD. CHIEF OkFICE : HOLBORN BARS, LONDON. SUMMARY of the REPORTS presented at the FORTY SIXTH ANNUAL MEETING held on 7th March, 1894. ORDINARY BRANCH.—The number of Policies issued during the year was 61,744, assuring the sum of £6,282,120, and producing a New Annual Premium Income of £339,957. The number of Deaths was 3,584.an1 198 Endowment Assurances matured. The number of policies in force at the end of the year was 375.545 INDUSTRIAL BRANC H.—The Premiums received during the year were £4,244.224, being an increase of £272,360. The number of Free Policies grAnted during the year to those Policyholders of five years' standing, who desired t.) discontinue their payments, was 66,478, the number in fore. being 398,078. About a fortnight since I left my work—l commenced my illness by feeling starved, and a nasty stinging and shooting pain in my head; a doctor was called in, and he said it was the Influenza, and I suffered for over a week of the pains in my head, and the doctor's medicine did me Tv) good, till at last the mistress of this house (I am in lodgings nere) fell ill with the same symptoms of illness till her husband advised us to try a bottle of your Bitters. He got a is. lid. bottle, and I may toll you the first &se gave us xeliet. After this we got a 2s. 9d. bottle, and now we are all right and I am at my work now, and I have no doubt that if I had taken the Bitters at first I should have lost no work, and I owe my recovery to the bene- ficial virtues of your medicine, and I beg to tender to you my sincere thanks for producing such a valuable medicine to suffering humanity. I shall recommend it to all my friends, and I cannot speak too highly of it, as it is worth its weight in gold. (Signed) JAMES SAGAR. THE LIBERATOR SOCIETY, - • NO HOPE FOR 'THE SHAREHOLDERS. A special meeting of the shareholders of the Liberator Society was held in London on Monday to receive a statement from Mr. Wheeler, the official receiver. He informed the meeting that a bill for obtaining certain c•mpolsory powers for the purchase of properties in the Strand in front ol the large hotel now in course of erec- tion lied been lodged by him as liquidator of the Steiety, and it was necessary at that stage to obtain a formal resolution of the,membas of the Society approving of the course lie had adopted. In a brief explanation as to the present position of the affairs of the Society,Mr. Wheeler said he should be failing in his duty if he held -mt to them any hope whatever of their recovering any part of their investments. The claims of depositors and creditors which ranked in front of the shareholders' claims arnqunted to nearly £2.000.000. It was, he feared, quite clear that under the most favourable realisation no surplus could he available for tlo shareholders. The formal resolution approvitig of the bill to be prionoted lin Parliainentt authorising the widening cl the Strand I was agreed to, with only five dis,entients.
